The cancellation property for projective modules over integral group rings
John Nicholson

Abstract
. We obtain a partial classification of the finite groups G G for which the integral group ring ℤ ​ G \mathbb{Z}G has projective cancellation, i.e. for which P ⊕ ℤ ​ G ≅ Q ⊕ ℤ ​ G P\oplus\mathbb{Z}G\cong Q\oplus\mathbb{Z}G implies P ≅ Q P\cong Q for projective ℤ ​ G \mathbb{Z}G -modules P P and Q Q . In particular, we determine when projective cancellation holds for a finite group with no exceptional binary polyhedral quotients. To do this, we prove a cancellation theorem based on a relative version of the Eichler condition. We then use a group theoretic argument to precisely determine the class of groups not covered by this result. The final classification is then obtained by applying results of Swan, Chen and Bley-Hofmann-Johnston which show failure of projective cancellation for certain groups.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 20C05, 20C10; Secondary 19B28.

1.
Introduction

Let
G
be a finite group, let
\mathbb{Z}G
denote the integral group ring and recall that a
\mathbb{Z}G
-lattice
is a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module whose underlying abelian group is
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
for some
n
. This can equivalently be viewed as an integral representation
G\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Consider the following
cancellation problem
: for
\mathbb{Z}G
-lattices
M
,
N
, when does
M\oplus\mathbb{Z}G\cong N\oplus\mathbb{Z}G
imply
M\cong N
?
This is grounded in fundamental problems in both topology and number theory.
Its resolution, for certain
\mathbb{Z}G
-lattices, is essential in the classification of finite 2-complexes up to homotopy equivalence
[
Joh03
,
Nic21b
]
, closed 4-manifolds up to homeomorphism
[
HK88a
,
HK88b
]
, and in determining when a number field has a normal integral basis
[
Tay81
]
.
These applications have so far only been obtained for particular finite groups
G
, and the apparent intractability of the cancellation problem over arbitrary finite groups presents an obstacle to extending such results to a more general setting.

We say that
\mathbb{Z}G
, or the group
G
, has
projective cancellation
(PC) if
M\oplus\mathbb{Z}G\cong N\oplus\mathbb{Z}G
implies
M\cong N
for all finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules
M
and
N
.
This is equivalent to asking that finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules are determined by their image in the K-group
K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
.
This is the simplest case of the cancellation problem for
\mathbb{Z}G
-lattices and is of particular significance since projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules arise naturally in many of the applications (e.g.
[
Tay81
]
) and PC often implies cancellation for other
\mathbb{Z}G
-lattices (e.g.
[
Nic20
,
Nic21b
]
).

This was first studied by Swan
[
Swa60a
]
who showed that PC fails for the quaternion group
Q_{32}
of order 32
[
Swa62
]
.
Jacobinski
[
Jac68
]
showed that
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC if
G
satisfies the
Eichler condition
, which asks that the Wedderburn decomposition of
\mathbb{R}G
has no copies of the quaternions
\mathbb{H}=M_{1}(\mathbb{H})
. This holds if and only if
G
has no quotient which is a
binary polyhedral group
, i.e. a quaternion group
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 2
, or one of the exceptional groups
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{I}
which are the binary tetrahedral, octahedral and icosahedral groups.
Fröhlich
[
Frö75
]
showed that, if
G
has a quotient
H
and
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC, then
\mathbb{Z}H
has PC.
Finally Swan
[
Swa83
]
showed that, if
G
is a binary polyhedral group, then PC fails for
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
and holds otherwise. This leaves open the case where
G
has a quotient of the form

Q_{8}
,
Q_{12}
,
Q_{16}
,
Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}

but no quotient of the form
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
.

In this article, we will introduce a method for dealing with this remaining family of groups.
We also consider
stably free cancellation
(SFC) which asks that cancellation holds for all
M
,
N
free, i.e. stably free
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules are free.
The following is a special case of the results we obtain.

Theorem A
.

Let
G
be a finite group such that
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbb{Z}G
has projective cancellation

(ii)

\mathbb{Z}G
has stably free cancellation

(iii)

G
has no quotients of the form
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
,
Q_{4n}\times C_{2}
for
2\leq n\leq 5
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
or
G_{(100,7)}
.

Here
G_{(n,m)}
denotes the
m
th group of order
n
in GAP’s Small Groups library
[
BEO02
,
GAP
]
,
C_{n}
denotes the cyclic group of order
n
, and
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptH
means that
H
is not a quotient of
G
.

Remark 1.1
.

(a) The previous results summarised above determine PC and SFC for finite groups
G
such that
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8}
,
Q_{12}
,
Q_{16}
,
Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{I}
. For such groups,
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC if and only if
\mathbb{Z}G
has SFC if and only if
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{4n}
for all
n\geq 6
.
Theorem

A
can be viewed as an extension of this result whereby we exclude just three binary polyhedral quotients rather than seven.

(b) A more general statement holds, though is more complicated to state.
Firstly, we can replace the condition
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}
with the much weaker condition that
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(96,66)}
,
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
or
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
(see
Section

7
for a definition) for
n\geq 1
. We would then need to add
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
to the list in (iii). This follows from
Theorem

7.1
and
Fig.

4
.
Secondly, we have that (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) for an arbitrary finite group
G
.

(c) If
G
is a finite 2-group, then
G
has no quotient of the form
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}
since
3\mid|\widetilde{T}|,|\widetilde{O}|,|\widetilde{I}|
. For such groups,
Theorem

A
implies that
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC if and only if
\mathbb{Z}G
has SFC if and only if
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
or
Q_{2^{n}}
for
n\geq 5
.
This determines PC and SFC for almost all finite groups subject to thevconjecture that almost all finite groups are
2
-groups.
For example, more than 99% of groups of order at most 2000 are of order 1024
[
BEO02
]
(see also
[
Bur22
]
).

We will also establish the following. This applies to different groups to
Theorem

A
. For example,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
is covered by this result whilst
G_{(36,7)}
is not.

Theorem B
.

Let
G
be a finite group such that
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbb{Z}G
has projective cancellation

(ii)

\mathbb{Z}G
has stably free cancellation

(iii)

G
has no quotients of the form
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
even,
Q_{4n}\times C_{2}
for
n=2,4
or
n\geq 3
odd,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
or
G_{(64,14)}
.

Remark 1.2
.

(a) If
G\twoheadrightarrow Q_{8}
or
Q_{16}
, then
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}
and so
Theorem

B
completely determines whether such a group
G
has PC or SFC respectively (without the assumption that
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{I}
). If
G\twoheadrightarrow Q_{8}
, then
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC (or SFC) if and only if
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8}\times C_{2}
or
G_{(32,14)}
. If
G\twoheadrightarrow Q_{16}
, then
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC (or SFC) if and only if
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{16}\times C_{2},G_{(32,14)}
or
G_{(64,14)}
.

(b) We have that (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) for an arbitrary finite group
G
.

(c) If
G
is a non-cyclic finite 2-group, then
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}
(see
[
Mil26
, p253]
). In particular, as in
Remark

1.1
(c),
Theorem

B
conjecturally determines PC and SFC for almost all finite groups.

Knowing that PC and SFC coincide in each of these cases is of interest since there are currently no known examples of a group ring
\mathbb{Z}G
where this is not the case.
Note that there exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-order in a semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra which has SFC but not PC
[
SV19
, Theorem 1.3]
.
We therefore ask:

Question 1.3
.

Does there exist a finite group
G
such that
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\SFC
but not
\PC
?

In the remainder of the introduction, we will introduce the method behind Theorems
A
and
B
as well as more general classification results which are discussed in
Section

1.3
.
The method is based on two key new ideas. The first is a relative version of Jacobinski’s theorem for quotients
G\twoheadrightarrow H
satisfying the
relative Eichler condition
(
Section

1.1
).
The second is the notion of
Eichler simple groups
and the
Fundamental Lemma
which makes it possible to determine the groups which are not covered by cancellation theorems based on the relative Eichler condition (
Section

1.2
).
The outcome of this approach is that, provided a given list of finite groups can be shown to fail PC (resp. SFC), we would obtain a complete classification of the finite groups with PC (resp. SFC).
Our main results are then obtained by using results of Swan
[
Swa83
]
, Chen
[
Che86
]
and Bley-Hofmann-Johnston
[
BHJ24
]
which show failure of SFC for a number of these groups (
Section

1.3
).

Conventions

From now on,
G
and
H
will denote finite groups. All groups will be considered up to group isomorphism.
We often state properties of
\mathbb{Z}G
as properties of
G
(e.g. ‘
G
has PC’).
We write
G\twoheadrightarrow H
(resp.
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptH
) to denote the statement that
H
is a quotient of
G
(resp.
H
is not a quotient of
G
).
For a ring
R
, all
R
-modules will be taken to be finitely generated left
R
-modules.

1.1.
The relative Eichler condition and cancellation lifting

Let
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
denote the number of copies of the quaternions
\mathbb{H}=M_{1}(\mathbb{H})
in the Wedderburn decomposition of
\mathbb{R}G
, so that
G
satisfies the Eichler condition if and only if
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=0
.
We say that a pair of finite groups
(G,H)
satisfies the
relative Eichler condition
if
G
has a quotient
H
and
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
. When this holds, we say
H
is an
Eichler quotient
of
G
and that
G
is an
Eichler cover
of
H
. Otherwise, we say
H
is a
non-Eichler quotient
of
G
and that
G
is a
non-Eichler cover
of
H
.

We say that
\mathbb{Z}H
satisfies
PC lifting
(resp.
SFC lifting
) if
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC (resp. SFC) whenever
G
is an Eichler cover of
H
.
Note that this property entails that
\mathbb{Z}H
itself has PC (resp. SFC).

We will establish the following relative version of Jacobinski’s theorem
[
Jac68
]
.
This is a special case of a more general result which applies to
\mathbb{Z}
-orders in semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras (
Theorem

3.1
).

Theorem C
.

Let
H
be a finite group such that:

(i)

The map
\mathbb{Z}H^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}H)
is surjective

(ii)

Every finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}H
-module is left isomorphic to an ideal in
\mathbb{Z}H
which is generated by central elements.

If
\mathbb{Z}H
has
\PC
, then
\mathbb{Z}H
has
\PC
lifting, i.e. if
G
has an Eichler quotient
H
, then
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
.

This generalises
[
Nic21a
, Theorem A]
, which applied in the case of SFC and did not require condition (ii). The proof is based on the decomposition of
\mathbb{Z}G
as a pullback of rings
\mathbb{Z}H\times_{(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})[H]}\Lambda
(see
Lemma

4.1
). Condition (ii) implies that projective
\mathbb{Z}H
-modules are locally free as
(\mathbb{Z}H,\mathbb{Z}H)
-bimodules (
Section

2.2
).
The main technical tool is
Lemma

3.6
which shows that, modulo the action of locally free
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodules, an arbitrary projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
P
can be assumed to map to a free module over
\mathbb{Z}H
under extension of scalars.
Condition (i) then implies that the class
[P]
has cancellation via a Mayer-Vietoris argument (
Section

2.3
).

We would next like to use
Theorem

C
to establish PC for many of the groups which remain after the results of Jacobinski, Fröhlich and Swan.
Swan showed that two additional families of groups have PC:
H\times K
where

H\in\{Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20},\widetilde{T},\widetilde{O},\widetilde{I}\}

and
K\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptC_{2}

[
Swa83
, Theorem 15.5]
, and
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
for any
n,m\geq 0

[
Swa83
, Theorem 13.7]
.
The following recovers PC in both cases.

Theorem D
.

Let
G
be a finite group which has an Eichler quotient
H
of the form

(1)

C_{1},\,Q_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20},\,\widetilde{T},\,\widetilde{O},\,\widetilde{I}\quad\text{or}\quad\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\text{ for $n,m\geq 0$}.

Then
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
. That is, for each group
H
listed above,
\mathbb{Z}H
satisfies
\PC
lifting.

The proof is in two parts. Firstly, we establish PC lifting for
H\in\{Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20}\}
using
Theorem

C
. We verify (i) using the results of
[
MOV83
]
(see also
[
Nic21a
]
) and we verify (ii) by explicitly constructing projective ideals generated by central elements over each group (see
Proposition

4.4
). Secondly, the case
H=\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
for
n,m\geq 0
is dealt with using a cancellation theorem of Swan
[
Swa83
, Theorem 13.1]
. This result was previously used by Swan to establish PC for the groups themselves but, by verifying additional hypothesis, we show that it can be used to also establish PC lifting (see
Theorem

4.9
).
The following question remains open.

Question 1.4
.

Does every group with
\PC
satisfy
\PC
lifting?
That is, if
G
has an Eichler quotient
H
such that
\mathbb{Z}H
has
\PC
, then does
\mathbb{Z}G
always have
\PC
?

1.2.
Eichler simple groups the Fundamental Lemma

For a class of finite groups
S
, we say that
G
is
S
-Eichler
if
G
has an Eichler quotient
H
for some
H\in S
.
For example,
Theorem

D
says that
S
-Eichler implies PC where
S
is the list of groups in
Eq.

1
.
We say that a finite group
G
is
Eichler simple
if it has no proper Eichler quotients.
Examples include
C_{1}
, binary polyhedral groups,
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
for
n,m\geq 0
,
H\times C_{2}
for
H
a binary polyhedral group, and the groups listed in
Theorem

A
(iii) such as
G_{(32,14)}
. We say that a class of Eichler simple groups
S
is
closed under quotients
if
G\in S
and
G\twoheadrightarrow H
for
H
an Eichler simple group implies that
H\in S
.
We will show:

Lemma 1.5

(Fundamental Lemma)
.

Let
S
be a class of Eichler simple groups which is closed under quotients.
Then a finite group
G
is
S
-Eichler if and only if
G
has no quotient in
\MNEC(S)
.

Here
\MNEC(S)
denotes the class of
minimal non-Eichler covers
of the set
S
(see
Section

5.2
for a definition).
We use this to establish the following, which is our main group theoretic result.

Theorem E
.

Let
G
be a finite group. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

G
has an Eichler quotient
H
of the form:

C_{1},\,Q_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20},\,\widetilde{T},\,\widetilde{O},\,\widetilde{I}\quad\text{or}\quad\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\text{ for $n,m\geq 0$}

(ii)

G
has no quotient
H
of the form:

(a)

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6

(b)

Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
for
n\geq 1

(c)

Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
,
G_{(96,66)}
,
G_{(100,7)}
,
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
.

The semidirect product
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
is defined in
Section

7
.
The proof is based on applying the Fundamental Lemma to the list of groups
S
in
Eq.

1
.
We show that the groups in
S
are all Eichler simple and that
S
is closed under quotients. It remains to show that
\MNEC(S)
consists of the groups in (ii) (a)-(c).
The calculation is broken into two parts. Firstly, we compute
\MNEC(H)
for
H\in\{C_{1},Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20},\widetilde{T},\widetilde{O},\widetilde{I}\}
by means of an algorithm (Algorithm
6.7
) which we implement in GAP
[
GAP
]
and Magma
[
BCP97
]
(
Section

6
).
Secondly, we calculate
\MNEC(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
using a range of methods such as classifying extensions using group cohomology (
Section

7
).

1.3.
Consequences for the classification of finite groups with PC

An immediate consequence of Theorems
D
and
E
is that we obtain a potential strategy to completely classify the finite groups
G
for which
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
.
In particular, by
Theorem

D
, the groups in
Theorem

E
(i) all have
\PC
. Hence, if the groups listed in
Theorem

E
(ii) (a)-(c) all failed PC, then the finite groups with PC would be precisely those which satisfy the hypotheses in
Theorem

E
.

Previously, PC was known to fail for
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
and
Q_{8}\times C_{2}
by Swan
[
Swa83
]
and for
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(36,7)}
and
G_{(100,7)}
by Chen
[
Che86
]
.
In March 2022, we sent the list of groups (a)-(c) to Bley-Hofmann-Johnston with the hope that the eight remaining groups in (c) and at least the smallest group
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
in (b) would fail PC.
They showed the following
[
BHJ24
]
:

Theorem 1.6

(Bley-Hofmann-Johnston)
.

\SFC
fails for
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
and
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}^{2}
.

\SFC
holds for
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(96,66)}
,
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
, as well as
G_{(192,183)}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{20}
.

This determines SFC for all groups listed in
Theorem

E
(ii) except
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
and
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
for any
n\geq 2
. The proof made use of computer calculations which become harder for larger groups.
We will now deduce
Theorem

A
from Theorems
D
,
E
and
1.6
.

Proof of
Theorem

A
.

Suppose
G\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}
. If
G
has an Eichler quotient in
Eq.

1
, then
Theorem

D
implies
G
has
\PC
. If not, then
Theorem

E
implies
G
has a quotient
H
which is one of the groups in
Theorem

E
(ii) (a)-(c).
The groups
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
for
n\geq 1
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(96,66)}
,
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
all have quotients in
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}
(see
Fig.

4
).
By
[
Swa83
]
,
[
Che86
]
and
Theorem

1.6
,
\SFC
fails for all remaining groups
H
and so fails for
G
. The result follows.
∎

We conclude by discussing the finite groups for which PC (resp. SFC) remains open.
If
G
has an Eichler quotient which is one the groups in
Eq.

1
, then
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC. If not,
G
has a quotient which is one of the groups in
Theorem

E
(ii). By results of Swan, Chen and Bley-Hofmann-Johnston,
G
fails SFC if it has a quotient which is one of the groups in:

(a)

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6

(c)
′

Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
,
G_{(100,7)}
.

This leaves open the case where
G
has a quotient of the form
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(96,66)}
,
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
or
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
for
n\geq 1
, but no quotient of the form (a) or (c)
′
.

Whilst
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(96,66)}
and
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
all have SFC (by
Theorem

1.6
), the possibility remains that they fail PC.
However, using
Theorem

1.6
, we will show:

Theorem 1.7
.

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has
\PC
. In particular, there exists a finite group with PC which does not satisfy the hypotheses in
Theorem

E
.

This demonstrates that the classification of finite groups with PC suggested by
Theorem

E
is not complete.
We can attempt to remedy this via the following, which is an analogue of
Theorem

E
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
G
be a finite group such that
G\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

G
has an Eichler quotient
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}

(ii)

G
has no quotient of the form

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
,

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
,
(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T})\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}\times\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{T}^{2}\times C_{2}
.

If we could show that
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
had PC lifting, then we would have completed the classification of finite groups with PC provided the following groups all fail PC:
G_{(96,66)}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
,
(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T})\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}\times\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{T}^{2}\times C_{2}
and
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
for
n\geq 1
.
This gives an iterative process whereby, if one of these groups has PC, we could prove a result such as
Theorem

1.8
above and replace the group with several others.
Provided that PC can be determined for the infinite family
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
, and all groups have PC lifting, this has the potential to reduce the complete classification of the finite groups with PC to finite computation.
At present, we know that
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has
\SFC
lifting (
Proposition

4.14
) but PC lifting remains unknown (
Question

4.16
). By
Theorem

1.6
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
and
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{20}
have SFC but the possibility remains that they fail PC.

In
Section

8
, we establish a number of additional partial classifications. Using Theorem
E
, we study groups with
|G|
bounded and
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
bounded respectively (
Section

8.2
). We prove
Theorem

B
, which involves establishing an analogue of Theorem
E
for groups which have a
C_{2}^{2}
quotient (
Section

8.3
).
Finally, we reflect on the applications of projective cancellation to topology given in
[
Nic21b
,
Nic20
]
in the case where
G
has periodic cohomology (
Section

8.4
). Our results lead to new and simpler proofs of the results obtained in those articles.

Organisation of the paper

The paper will be structured into three parts.
In
Part

I
, we develop the theory of cancellation for locally free
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules and establish Theorems
C
and
D
.

In
Part

II
, we introduce and study the class of Eichler simple groups
\mathscr{E}
. We prove the Fundamental Lemma (
Lemma

1.5
).
We give algorithms for computing
\MNEC(H)
for a group
H
.

In
Part

III
, the main computations are carried out. We calculate
\MNEC(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
(without a computer program). The results of our computer calculations are presented in a table in Appendix
A
.
Using both parts, we complete the proof of Theorem
E
.

Part I
Cancellation and the relative Eichler condition

The goal of this part will be to establish Theorems
C
and
D
from the introduction.
Our approach will be to prove a general relative cancellation for
\mathbb{Z}
-orders in finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras (
Theorem

3.1
).
In
Section

2
, we develop the theory of locally free modules over orders. Much of this is taken from Swan
[
Swa80
,
Swa83
]
and Fröhlich
[
Frö73
]
, but we offer slight variations on these results throughout (particularly

Proposition

2.5
,

Corollary

2.12
,
Lemma

2.16
and
Theorem

2.17
) which we believe have not previously appeared in this form.
In
Section

3
, we then establish
Theorem

3.1
, our main cancellation theorem for orders. In

Section

4
, we specialise to the case
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
and prove Theorems
C
and
D
.

If
M
is an
R
-module and
f:R\to S
is a ring homomorphism, we will write the
S
-module which is image of
M
under extension of scalars as
S\otimes M
,
S\otimes_{R}M
or
f_{\#}(M)
.

2.
Locally free modules and fibre squares

We will now give a brief summary of the theory of locally free modules over orders in finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras.
Throughout this section, let
A
be a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra and let
\Lambda
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-order in
A
, i.e. a subring of
A
which is finitely generated as an abelian group under addition and is such that
\mathbb{Q}\cdot\Lambda=A
. For example, we can take
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
and
A=\mathbb{Q}G
for a finite group
G
.

2.1.
Preliminaries on locally free modules and fibre squares

For a prime
p
, let
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
denote the
p
-adic integers and let
\Lambda_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
. We say a
\Lambda
-module
M
is
locally projective
if
M_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M
is a projective
\Lambda_{p}
-module for all primes
p
.
The following is well-known.

Proposition 2.1

(
[
Swa80
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Let
M
be a
\Lambda
-module. Then
M
is projective if and only if
M
is locally projective.

Similarly, we say that
M
is
locally free (of rank
n
)
if there exists
n\geq 1
for which
M_{p}
is a free
\Lambda_{p}
-module of rank
n
for all
p
prime.
In the special case where
A=\mathbb{Q}G
and
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
for a finite group
G
, we have the following refinement of Proposition
2.1
.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
Swa80
, p156]
)
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module. Then
M
is projective if and only if
M
is locally free.

Define the
locally free class group

C(\Lambda)
to be the set of equivalence classes of locally free
\Lambda
-modules up to the relation
P\sim Q
if
P\oplus\Lambda^{i}\cong Q\oplus\Lambda^{j}
for some
i,j\geq 0
. By abuse of notation, we write
[P]
to denote both the class
[P]\in C(\Lambda)
and, where convenient, the set of isomorphism classes of locally free modules
P_{0}
where
[P_{0}]=[P]
.

We also define the
class set

\Cls(\Lambda)
as the collection of isomorphism classes of rank one locally free
\Lambda
-modules, which is a finite set by the Jordan-Zassenhaus theorem
[
CR81
, Section 24]
. This is often written as
\LF_{1}(\Lambda)
(see
[
Swa83
]
).
This comes with the stable class map

[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}:\Cls(\Lambda)\to C(\Lambda),\quad P\mapsto[P].

This map is always surjective due to the following.
This was proven by A. Fröhlich in
[
Frö75
]
using idelic methods, generalising the case
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
first obtained by Swan
[
Swa60a
, Theorem A]
. However, it is worth noting that the first part follows already from the cancellation theorems of Bass and Serre
[
Swa80
, Section 2]
.

Proposition 2.3

(
[
Frö75
, p115]
)
.

Let
M
is a locally free
\Lambda
-module. Then there exists a left ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda
which is a rank one locally free
\Lambda
-module and such that
M\cong I\oplus\Lambda^{i}
for some
i\geq 0
.

We say that
\Lambda
has
locally free cancellation
(LFC) if
P\oplus\Lambda\cong Q\oplus\Lambda
implies
P\cong Q
for all locally free
\Lambda
-modules
P
and
Q
. By
Proposition

2.3
, we have that
\Lambda
has LFC if and only if
[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}
is bijective, i.e
|\Cls(\Lambda)|=|C(\Lambda)|
. When
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
, this coincides with projective cancellation (PC).

More generally, we say that a class
[P]\in C(\Lambda)
has cancellation if
P_{1}\oplus\Lambda\cong P_{2}\oplus\Lambda
implies
P_{1}\cong P_{2}
for all
P_{1},P_{2}\in[P]
.
We say that
\Lambda
has
stably free cancellation (SFC)
when
[\Lambda]
has cancellation, i.e. when every stably free
\Lambda
-module is free.
We write
\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)=[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}^{-1}([P])
and
\SF(\Lambda)=\Cls^{[\Lambda]}(\Lambda)
so that, by
Proposition

2.3
,
[P]\in C(\Lambda)
has cancellation if and only if
|\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)|=1
.

Recall that
[P]\in C(\Lambda)
can be represented as a graded tree with vertices the isomorphism classes of non-zero modules
P_{0}\in[P]
, edges between each
P_{0}\in[P]
and
P_{0}\oplus\Lambda\in[P]
and with grading from the rank of each locally free
\Lambda
-module.
By
Proposition

2.3
, this takes the following simple form where the set of minimal vertices corresponds to
\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)
(see
Fig.

1
).

\vdots

Figure 1.
Tree structure for
[P]\in C(\Lambda)

The following is a consequence of a general cancellation theorem of Jacobinski. The proof depends on deep results of Eichler on strong approximation
[
Eic37
]
.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
Jac68
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Suppose
\Lambda
satisfies the Eichler condition. Then
\Lambda
has LFC. In particular,
\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)=\{P\}
for all
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
.

We will now observe that locally free
\Lambda
-modules cannot be detected on
A
or on any finite ring quotients of
\Lambda
. For example, if
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
, then
M\in\Cls(\mathbb{Z}G)
has
\mathbb{Q}\otimes M\cong\mathbb{Q}G
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes M\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}G
. More generally, we have the following.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
M
be a locally free
\Lambda
-module.
Then
\mathbb{Q}\otimes M
is a free
A
-module and, if
f:\Lambda\to\bar{\Lambda}
is a surjective ring homomorphism for a finite ring
\bar{\Lambda}
, then
\bar{\Lambda}\otimes M
is a free
\bar{\Lambda}
-module.

The result over
\mathbb{Q}
is a consequence of the Noether-Deuring theorem and can be found in
[
FRU74
, p. 407]
.
The result over finite rings is known
[
RU74
, Remark 4.9]
though we were not able to locate a proof in the literature except the case
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G

[
Swa60a
, Theorem 7.1]
, so we include one below.

Proof.

We will start by considering the case
\bar{\Lambda}=\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
.
First note that
\Lambda/n\cong(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
. In particular, if
M
is a
\Lambda
-module, then
M/n\cong(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M
can be viewed as a
\Lambda/n
-module.

By Proposition
2.3
, it suffices to consider the case where
M
is locally free of rank one. By Proposition
2.1
, we have that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M\cong\Lambda_{p}
for all
p
prime. Since
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we have that

\displaystyle M/p

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M)

\displaystyle\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Lambda_{p}\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\cong\Lambda/p.

Let
a_{p}\in M
be such that
[a_{p}]\in M/p
maps to
1\in\Lambda/p
under this isomorphism. As in the proof of Hensel’s lemma, we can check that
\Lambda/(p^{k})\to M/(p^{k})
,
1\mapsto[a_{p}]
is an isomorphism for all
k\geq 1

If
n=p_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots p_{r}^{k_{r}}
is a factorisation into distinct primes, then the Chinese remainder theorem implies that there exists
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\alpha_{i}\equiv 1
mod
p_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}\equiv 0
mod
p_{j}
for
i\neq j
. By the argument above, there exists
a_{i}\in M
such that the map
1\mapsto[a_{i}]
gives an isomorphism
\Lambda/p^{k_{i}}\to M/p^{k_{i}}
. If
a=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}a_{i}
, then
\Lambda/n\to M/n
,
1\mapsto a
is an isomorphism, as required.

Now let
\bar{\Lambda}
be an arbitrary finite ring.
Note that
\bar{\Lambda}=\Lambda/I
for a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda
and, since
\Lambda
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-order in
A
, we have that
\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\Lambda
. Since
\bar{\Lambda}
is finite,
I\subseteq\Lambda
must have finite index and so
I\cap\mathbb{Z}=(n)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
for some
n\geq 1
. In particular, this implies that
n\Lambda\subseteq I
and so there is a composition
\Lambda\to\Lambda/n\to\Lambda/I
.
Hence, if
M
is a locally free
\Lambda
-module, then
\bar{\Lambda}\otimes M\cong\bar{\Lambda}\otimes(M/n)
. Since
M/n
is a free
\Lambda/n
-module by the case
\bar{\Lambda}=\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
above,
\bar{\Lambda}\otimes M
must be a free
\bar{\Lambda}
-module.
∎

Let
\Lambda
,
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
be
\mathbb{Z}
-orders in finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras
A
,
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
respectively, let
\widebar{\Lambda}
be a finite ring and suppose there is a Milnor square:

\mathcal{R}=\hbox to84.08pt{\vbox to51.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.04161pt\lower-24.65279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -16.33 -23.51 L 20.22 -23.51}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.81528pt}{-16.99306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 20.5 -23.51)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.20056pt}{-13.2792pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.04 -18.37)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Since
\widebar{\Lambda}
is a finite ring, we have that
\mathbb{Q}\otimes\widebar{\Lambda}=0
. Since
\mathbb{Q}
is a flat module, tensoring the above diagram with
\mathbb{Q}
gives another pullback diagram which implies that the map

\mathbb{Q}\otimes(i_{1},i_{2}):\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Lambda\to(\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Lambda_{1})\times(\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Lambda_{2})

is an isomorphism, i.e.
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
.

For a ring
R
, recall that

K_{1}(R)=\GL(R)^{\text{ab}}

where
\GL(R)=\bigcup_{n}\GL_{n}(R)
with respect to the inclusions
\GL_{n}(R)\hookrightarrow\GL_{n+1}(R)
.
The following is a consequence of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for
\mathcal{R}
combined with
Proposition

2.5
. This is proven in
[
RU74
, 6.2]
.

Proposition 2.6
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is as above, then there is an exact sequence

K_{1}(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{}K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})\times K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})\xrightarrow{}K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})\xrightarrow{\partial}C(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{}C(\Lambda_{1})\times C(\Lambda_{2})\xrightarrow{}0

where
\partial
is the snake map and all other maps are functorial.

We will now give general conditions under which we can relate cancellation over two orders
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
when there is a map
f:\Lambda_{1}\to\Lambda_{2}
.
The following was shown by Swan and generalises an earlier result of Fröhlich
[
Frö75
, VIII]
.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
Swa83
, Theorem A10]
)
.

Let
f:\Lambda_{1}\to\Lambda_{2}
be a map of
\mathbb{Z}
-orders in a semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
such that the induced map
f_{*}:\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda_{2}
is surjective.
Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cls(\Lambda_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cls(\Lambda_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(\Lambda_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(\Lambda_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\#}}
\scriptstyle[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda_{1}}
\scriptstyle[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\#}}

is a weak pullback square with all maps surjective.

In particular, if
P_{1}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{1})
and
P_{2}=f_{\#}(P_{1})\in\Cls(\Lambda_{2})
, then this implies that the map

f_{\#}:\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})\to\Cls^{[P_{2}]}(\Lambda_{2})

is surjective. Hence, if
[P_{1}]
has cancellation, then
[P_{2}]
has cancellation.

Let
G
be a finite group with quotient
H
. The situation of
Theorem

2.7
arises when
\Lambda_{1}=\mathbb{Z}G
,
\Lambda_{2}=\mathbb{Z}H
and
f:\mathbb{Z}G\to\mathbb{Z}H
is induced by the quotient map and is itself surjective.
This implies that the properties PC (i.e. LFC) and SFC are closed under quotients of groups.

2.2.
Central Picard groups

We will now consider the question of when a locally free
\Lambda
-module can be represented by a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda
and so has the additional structure of a bimodule. This was first considered by Fröhlich
[
Frö73
]
and Fröhlich-Reiner-Ullom
[
FRU74
]
.

Recall that, for a ring
R
, an
(R,R)
-bimodule
M
is
invertible
if there exists an
(R,R)
-bimodule
N
and bimodules isomorphisms

f:M\otimes_{R}N\to R,\quad g:N\otimes_{R}M\to R

such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{R}N\otimes_{R}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes_{R}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{R}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes g}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{R}M\otimes_{R}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes_{R}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{R}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes f}

The
central Picard group

\Picent(R)
is the group of
(R,R)
-bimodule isomorphism classes of
(R,R)
-bimodules
M
for which
xm=mx
for all
m\in M
and central elements
x\in Z(R)
.

In the special case where
R=\Lambda
is an order in a semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, the central Picard group has the following basic properties.

Firstly, let
I(\Lambda)
denote the multiplicative group of two-sided ideals
I\subseteq\Lambda
which are invertible in the sense that there exists a fractional two-sided ideal
J\subseteq\mathbb{Q}\cdot\Lambda
for which
I\cdot J=\Lambda
. If
I\in I(\Lambda)
, then it follows that
I
is invertible as a
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodule and
xm=mx
for all
m\in I
,
x\in Z(\Lambda)
. The following can be found in
[
CR87
, Corollary 55.18]
.

Theorem 2.8
.

There is an isomorphism of abelian groups:

\Picent(\Lambda)\cong I(\Lambda)/\{\Lambda a:a\in(\mathbb{Q}\cdot C)^{\times}\}

where
C=Z(\Lambda)
is the centre of
\Lambda
.

In particular, every
M\in\Picent(\Lambda)
is bimodule isomorphic to an invertible two-sided ideal. If
I
,
J\in I(\Lambda)
, then
I\cong J
as
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodules if and only if there exists
a\in(\mathbb{Q}\cdot C)^{\times}
such that
I=Ja
.

The following is a consequence of combining this with
[
CR87
, Proposition 55.29]
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
. Then there exists
J\subseteq\Lambda
two-sided with
J\in\Cls(\Lambda)
such that
I\otimes_{\Lambda}J\cong\Lambda\cong J\otimes_{\Lambda}I
as
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodules. In particular,
I\in I(\Lambda)
.

In particular this shows that, if
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
, then
I
induces a bijection
I\otimes_{\Lambda}-:\Cls(\Lambda)\to\Cls(\Lambda)
.
We can therefore consider an even stronger notion of local freeness than for left modules.
We say that a
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodule
M
is
locally free as a bimodule
if there exists
i\geq 1
such that, for all
p
prime,
M_{p}\cong\Lambda_{p}^{i}
are isomorphic as
(\Lambda_{p},\Lambda_{p})
-bimodules.
We will now need the following two closely related results.

Proposition 2.10

(
[
CR87
, Proposition 55.16]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative Noetherian local ring and let
\Lambda
be a commutative finitely generated
R
-algebra. Then
\Picent(\Lambda)=1
.

In particular, if
\Lambda
is an
\mathbb{Z}
-order in a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
and
C=Z(\Lambda)
, then
C_{p}
is a commutative finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra and
C_{(p)}
is a commutative finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-algebra. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
are both Noetherian, this implies that
\Picent(C_{p})=1
and
\Picent(C_{(p)})=1
.

The following was shown by Fröhlich (see also
[
CR87
, Theorem 55.25]
).
Note that, since
\tau^{\prime}\circ\tau
factors
\Picent(C_{p})
, the fact that
\tau^{\prime}\circ\tau=0
follows from
\Picent(C_{p})=1
.

Theorem 2.11

(
[
Frö73
, Theorem 6]
)
.

For all but finitely many primes
p
, we have
\Picent(\Lambda_{p})=1
and there is an exact sequence

1\to\Picent(C)\xrightarrow{\tau}\Picent(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\tau^{\prime}}\prod_{p}\Picent(\Lambda_{p})\to 1

where
C=Z(\Lambda)
is the centre of
\Lambda
and
\tau(M)=\Lambda\otimes_{C}M
for
M\in\Picent(C)
.

This leads to the following three equivalent characterisations of locally free bimodules. This is presumably well-known, though we were not able to locate an equivalent statement in the literature.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

I
is generated by central elements

(ii)

I
is locally free as a bimodule, i.e. for all
p
prime,
I_{p}\cong\Lambda_{p}
are isomorphic as
(\Lambda_{p},\Lambda_{p})
-bimodules.

(iii)

For all
p
prime,
I_{(p)}\cong\Lambda_{(p)}
are isomorphic as
(\Lambda_{(p)},\Lambda_{(p)})
-bimodules.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.9
,
I
represents a class
[I]\in\Picent(\Lambda)
. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) now follows from
Theorem

2.8
and
Theorem

2.11
.

Now, (iii) implies (ii) since
\Lambda_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}\Lambda_{(p)}
.
In order to show that (i) implies (iii), suppose that
I
is generated by central elements and, for
p
prime, let
\tau^{\prime\prime}:\Picent(\Lambda)\to\Picent(\Lambda_{(p)})
be the induced map. Then there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Picent(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Picent(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Picent(C_{(p)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Picent(\Lambda_{(p)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\prime\prime}}

where all maps are the induced maps.
By
Proposition

2.10
, we have that
\Picent(C_{(p)})=1
and so
\tau^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau=0
. Since
I
is generated by central elements,
[I]\in\IM(\tau)
and so

[I_{(p)}]=\tau^{\prime\prime}(I)=0\in\Picent(\Lambda_{(p)})

which implies that
I_{(p)}\cong\Lambda_{(p)}
as
(\Lambda_{(p)},\Lambda_{(p)})
-bimodules.
∎

2.3.
Cancellation over fibre squares

For each splitting
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras, let
\Lambda_{1}
,
\Lambda_{2}
be the projections onto
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
which are
\mathbb{Z}
-orders in
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
respectively. If
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda/I_{1}
,
\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda/I_{2}
and
\widebar{\Lambda}=\Lambda/(I_{1}+I_{2})
then, by
[

, Example 42.3]
, there is a pullback diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

since
I_{1}\cap I_{2}=\{0\}
. Since
\mathbb{Q}\otimes(i_{1},i_{2})
induces the isomorphism
A\to A_{1}\times A_{2}
, we must have that
\mathbb{Q}\otimes\widebar{\Lambda}=0
which implies that
\widebar{\Lambda}
is a finite ring.
We will write
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})
to denote the diagram induced by the splitting
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
. Here
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
are assumed to be subrings of
A
.

Consider the maps

\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}:\Cls(\Lambda)\to\Cls(\Lambda_{1})\times\Cls(\Lambda_{2}),\quad C_{\mathcal{R}}:C(\Lambda)\to C(\Lambda_{1})\times C(\Lambda_{2})

which are both induced by the extension of scalars maps
((i_{1})_{\#},(i_{2})_{\#})
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and let
P_{k}=(i_{k})_{\#}(P)\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
for
k=1,2
. Then
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}
restricts to a surjection

\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}:\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)\twoheadrightarrow\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})\times\Cls^{[P_{2}]}(\Lambda_{2})

and
[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}
restricts to a surjection

[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}:\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\twoheadrightarrow C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([P_{1}],[P_{2}]).

Proof.

Since
(i,j):\Lambda\to\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}
is a map of
\mathbb{Z}
-orders in
A
such that
\mathbb{Q}\otimes(i_{1},i_{2})
is an isomorphism,
Theorem

2.7
implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cls(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cls(\Lambda_{1})\times\Cls(\Lambda_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(\Lambda_{1})\times C(\Lambda_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}}
\scriptstyle[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}
\scriptstyle[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda_{1}}\times[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{R}}}

is a weak pullback in that
\Cls(\Lambda)
maps onto the pullback of the lower right corner. Hence the fibres of
[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}
and
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}
map onto the fibres of
[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda_{1}}\times[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda_{2}}
and
C_{\mathcal{R}}
respectively, as required.
∎

In order to determine when
[\,\cdot\,]
is bijective, i.e. when
\Lambda
has LFC, it is therefore useful to give the following explicit forms for the fibres of
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}
and
C_{\mathcal{R}}
respectively.

For a
\Lambda
-module
M
, let
\End_{\Lambda}(M)
be the ring of
\Lambda
-module homomorphisms
f:M\to M
and let
\Aut_{\Lambda}(M)=\End_{\Lambda}(M)^{\times}
be the subset of
\Lambda
-module isomorphisms
f:M\to M
. We will write
\End(M)
and
\Aut(M)
when
\Lambda
is clear from the context.
Since
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
are both surjective in our construction above,
\mathcal{R}
is a Milnor square. If
P_{i}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{i})
, then we also have that
(j_{k})_{\#}(P_{k})
is free by
Proposition

2.5
(ii). The following are now a consequence of
[
Mil71
, Theorems 2.1-2.3]
and
Proposition

2.6
respectively.

Proposition 2.14
.

For
k=1,2
, let
P_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
. Then there is an isomorphism of
\widebar{\Lambda}
-modules
\varphi_{k}:(j_{k})_{\#}(P_{k})\to\widebar{\Lambda}
and, for any such
\varphi_{k}
, there is a one-to-one correspondence

\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}\hskip-2.84526pt/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})\leftrightarrow\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})

where the maps
\Aut(P_{k})\to\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}
are defined via the map
\varphi_{k}(1\otimes-):P_{k}\to\widebar{\Lambda}
.

Proposition 2.15
.

For
k=1,2
, let
P_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
. Then there is a one-to-one correspondence

\frac{K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})}{K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})\times K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})}\leftrightarrow C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([P_{1}],[P_{2}]).

For
i=1,2
, Morita equivalence gives us maps

\psi_{i}:\Aut(P_{i})=\End(P_{i})^{\times}\to K_{1}(\End(P_{i}))\cong K_{1}(\Lambda_{i}).

By
[
Swa83
, Corollary A17]
, these maps fit into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut(P_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(\End(P_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(\Lambda_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(h_{\#})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(h)}

for
h=j_{1}
or
j_{2}
respectively, and so define a map

\Psi_{P_{1},P_{2}}:\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})\twoheadrightarrow\frac{K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})}{K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})\times K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})}

which can be shown to coincide with the map induced by
[\,\cdot\,]
under the equivalences given in Propositions
2.14
and
2.15
.
Hence, by the discussion earlier, we have that
\Lambda
has LFC if and only if
\Psi_{P_{1},P_{2}}
is a bijection for all
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
such that
P_{1}=(i_{1})_{\#}(P)
and
P_{2}=(i_{2})_{\#}(P)
for some
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
.

Now consider the constant
K_{\mathcal{R}}=|\frac{K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})}{K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})\times K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})}|
associated to
\mathcal{R}
. It follows from Lemma
2.13
that
[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}
is surjective and so
|\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})|\geq K_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
P_{1}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{1})
and
P_{2}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{2})
and suppose that

|\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})|=K_{\mathcal{R}}.

Then
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\cap\Cls^{[\widetilde{P}]}(\Lambda)|=1
for all
[\widetilde{P}]\in C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([P_{1}],[P_{2}])
.

Proof.

By Propositions
2.14
and
2.15
, we have that

|\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})|=|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})|,\quad K_{\mathcal{R}}=|C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([P_{1}],[P_{2}])|

and so
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})|=|C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([P_{1}],[P_{2}])|
by our hypothesis. By Lemma
2.13
, this implies that the map

[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}:\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\to C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([P_{1}],[P_{2}])

is a bijection. The result follows since
[\,\cdot\,]_{\Lambda}^{-1}([\widetilde{P}])=\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\cap\Cls^{[\widetilde{P}]}(\Lambda)
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the following, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be as above, let
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and let
P_{k}=(i_{k})_{\#}(P)\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
for
k=1,2
.
Suppose that
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})|=K_{\mathcal{R}}
for all
\widetilde{P}_{1}\in\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})
and
\widetilde{P}_{2}\in\Cls^{[P_{2}]}(\Lambda_{2})
.
Then
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}
induces a bijection

\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)\cong\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})\times\Cls^{[P_{2}]}(\Lambda_{2}).

Note that, subject to the hypothesis, this implies that
[P]\in C(\Lambda)
has cancellation if and only if
[P_{1}]\in C(\Lambda_{1})
and
[P_{2}]\in C(\Lambda_{2})
cancellation.

Proof.

Recall that, by Lemma
2.13
, there is a surjection

\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}\mid_{\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)}:\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)\to\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})\times\Cls^{[P_{2}]}(\Lambda_{2})

which has fibres
(\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}\mid_{\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)})^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})=\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})\cap\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)
. By Lemma
2.16
, we have that
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})\cap\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)|=1
for all
\widetilde{P}_{1}\in\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})
and
\widetilde{P}_{2}\in\Cls^{[P_{2}]}(\Lambda_{2})
and this implies that
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}\mid_{\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)}
is a bijection.
∎

3.
Main cancellation theorem for orders in semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras

As before, let
\Lambda
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-order in a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
, and let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})
denote the fibre square corresponding to a splitting
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras.

The aim of this section will be to prove the following which is our main cancellation theorem for orders in semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras.
For a ring
R
, the map
R^{\times}\to K_{1}(R)
is taken to be the composition
R^{\times}=\GL_{1}(R)\hookrightarrow\GL(R)\twoheadrightarrow K_{1}(R)
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and let
P_{1}=(i_{1})_{\#}(P)\in\Cls(\Lambda_{1})
. Suppose the following conditions are satisfied by
\mathcal{R}
:

(i)

\Lambda_{2}
satisfies the Eichler condition

(ii)

The map
\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\to K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})
is surjective

(iii)

Every
\widetilde{P}_{1}\in\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})
is represented by a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda_{1}
which is generated by central elements.

Then the map
(i_{1})_{\#}:\Cls^{[P]}(\Lambda)\to\Cls^{[P_{1}]}(\Lambda_{1})
is a bijection. In particular,
[P]\in C(\Lambda)
has cancellation if and only if
[P_{1}]\in C(\Lambda_{1})
has cancellation.

By
Corollary

2.12
, a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda_{1}
is locally free as a
(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{1})
-bimodule if and only if it is generated by central elements. In particular, hypothesis (iii) is satisfied if
I
is generated by central elements.
The proof will be given in
Section

3.4
and will depend on Lemmas
3.4
,
3.5
and
3.6
which roughly correspond to the three conditions in
Theorem

3.1
.
The first lemma is due to Swan, the second is due to the author
[
Nic21a
]
and the third has not been previously observed.

3.1.
The Eichler condition

The following is a consequence of
[
Swa83
, A17 and A18]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\Lambda
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-order in a semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, let
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
, let
\bar{\Lambda}
be a finite ring and let
f:\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\widebar{\Lambda}
be a surjective ring homomorphism. Then
f_{\#}(\Aut(P))\leq\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}
is a normal subgroup and the map
\Aut(P)\to K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})
induces an isomorphism

\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P)\cong K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})/\penalty K_{1}(\Lambda).

Remark 3.3
.

By
Theorem

2.17
, it can be shown that this implies
Theorem

2.4
.

We can apply this to the case where
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})
for a splitting of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be as above.
If
\Lambda_{2}
satisfies the Eichler condition then, for all
P_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
for
k=1,2
, there is a bijection

\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\cong\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},\Lambda_{2})
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

3.2
, there are isomorphisms
\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})\cong K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})/\penalty K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})\cong\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Lambda^{\times}
. This implies that there is a bijection

\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Aut(P_{2})\cong\Aut(P_{1})\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Lambda_{2}^{\times}

which is equivalent to
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\cong\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},\Lambda_{2})
by
Proposition

2.14
.
∎

3.2.
Unit representation for
K_{1}

The following observation was first made in
[
Nic21a
]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be as above and suppose:

(i)

\Lambda_{2}
satisfies the Eichler condition

(ii)

The map
\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\to K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})
is surjective.

Then
|\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Lambda_{2}^{\times}|=K_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof.

Since
m_{\mathbb{H}}(\Lambda_{2})=0
,
Theorem

3.2
implies that the map
\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}\to K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})
induces an isomorphism
\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Lambda_{2}^{\times}\cong K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})/\penalty K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})
.
The relevant maps fit into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{1}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\Lambda_{2}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})}{K_{1}(\Lambda_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and so
\IM(\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\to\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\Lambda_{2}^{\times})=\IM(K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})\to K_{1}(\widebar{\Lambda})/K_{1}(\Lambda_{2}))
since the map
\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\to K_{1}(\Lambda_{1})
is surjective. Hence we have
|\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\backslash\,\widebar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\penalty\Lambda_{2}^{\times}|=K_{\mathcal{R}}
.
∎

3.3.
Two-sided ideals over orders in semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras

The main result of this section is as follows. This gives a method of constructing locally free two-sided ideals over
\Lambda
from locally free two-sided ideals over the projections
\Lambda_{k}
subject to certain conditions.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be as above and, for
k=1,2
, suppose
I_{k}\subseteq\Lambda_{k}
is a two-sided ideal such that
I_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
and which is generated by central elements.
Then there exists a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda
with
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
such that

(i)

For
k=1,2
, there is a
(\Lambda_{k},\Lambda)
-bimodule isomorphism

(i_{k})_{\#}(I)\cong(I_{k})_{i_{k}}

(ii)

For all
P_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
for
k=1,2
, there is a bijection

I\otimes_{\Lambda}-:\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\to\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(I_{1}\otimes_{\Lambda_{1}}P_{1},I_{2}\otimes_{\Lambda_{2}}P_{2}).

Remark 3.7
.

(i) This actually holds under the weaker hypothesis that
(I_{k})_{(p)}\cong(\Lambda_{k})_{(p)}
are isomorphic as
((\Lambda_{k})_{(p)},(\Lambda_{k})_{(p)})
-bimodules for all primes
p\mid|\widebar{\Lambda}|
. However, since we cannot currently see an application for this in the
\mathbb{Z}G
case, we will restrict to the centrally generated case for simplicity.

(ii) This can be viewed as a generalisation of a construction of Gustafson-Roggenkamp
[
GR88
, p376-377]
which applies to the case
I_{k}=\Lambda_{k}
for
k=1,2
. Whilst we could similarly consider different
I
corresponding to units
\alpha\in Z(\widebar{\Lambda})^{\times}
, we will avoid this for simplicity.

We will begin by proving the following embedding result, which can be viewed as a generalisation of
[
Swa60a
, Theorem A]
to bimodules.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal generated by central elements such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
. Then, for all
n\neq 0
, there exists a two-sided ideal
J\subseteq\Lambda
such that
I\cong J
as
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodules and
J\cap\mathbb{Z}
is coprime to
(n)
.

Note that, by
Corollary

2.12
, the hypothesis is satisfied whenever
I
is locally free as a bimodule.
In order to prove this, we will need the following two lemmas.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
n\neq 0
be an integer and let
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and, for all
p\mid n
prime, there is a
(\Lambda_{(p)},\Lambda_{(p)})
-bimodule isomorphism
I_{(p)}\cong\Lambda_{(p)}
. Then there is a
(\Lambda/n,\Lambda/n)
-bimodule isomorphism
f:\Lambda/n\to I/n
,
1\mapsto[a]
for some
a\in Z(\Lambda)\cap I
.

Proof.

For each
p\mid n
prime, consider the bimodule isomorphism
f:\Lambda_{(p)}\to I_{(p)}
,
1\mapsto[a_{p}]
for some
a_{p}\in I_{(p)}
. There exists
m\neq 0
such that
ma_{p}\in I\subseteq I_{(p)}
and
f^{\prime}:\Lambda_{(p)}\to I_{(p)}
,
1\mapsto[ma_{p}]
is still a bimodule isomorphism, and so we can assume that
a_{p}\in I\subseteq I_{(p)}
.
Since
f
is a bimodule isomorphism, we have that
a_{p}\in Z(\Lambda_{(p)})
and so
a_{p}\in Z(\Lambda)\cap I
.

Now,
f
induces a bimodule isomorphism
\Lambda_{(p)}/p\cong I_{(p)}/p
. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}/p\cong\mathbb{Z}/p
, there are bimodule isomorphisms
\Lambda/p\cong\Lambda_{(p)}/p
and
I/p\cong I_{(p)}/p
and so there exists a bimodule isomorphism
f_{p}:\Lambda/p\to I/p
,
1\mapsto[a_{p}]
. It is straightforward to check that the map
\widetilde{f}_{p^{i}}:\Lambda/p^{i}\to I/p^{i}
,
1\mapsto[a_{p}]
is also a bimodule isomorphism for all
i\geq 1
.

In general, suppose
n=p_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots p_{k}^{n_{k}}
for distinct primes
p_{i}
, and integers
n_{i}\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
. By the Chinese remainder theorem,
\mathbb{Z}/n\cong\mathbb{Z}/p_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{Z}/p_{k}^{n_{k}}
. By tensoring with
\Lambda
or
I
, we see that there are bimodule isomorphisms
\Lambda/n\cong\Lambda/p_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Lambda/p_{k}^{n_{k}}
and
I/n\cong I/p_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times I/p_{k}^{n_{k}}
. Hence, by the bimodule isomorphism constructed above, there is a bimodule isomorphism
f:\Lambda/n\to I/n
,
1\mapsto[a]
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}\cdot\langle a_{p_{1}},\cdots,a_{p_{k}}\rangle\subseteq Z(\Lambda)\cap I
.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
n\neq 0
be an integer, let
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
, and let
f:\Lambda/n\to I/n
,
1\mapsto[a]
be a
(\Lambda/n,\Lambda/n)
-bimodule isomorphism for some
a\in Z(\Lambda)\cap I
. Then
\Lambda a\cong\Lambda
as a
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodule and there exists
m\neq 0
such that
mI\subseteq\Lambda a
and
(n,m)=1
.

Proof.

Since
a\in Z(\Lambda)
,
\Lambda a
is a bimodule and there is a map of bimodules
\varphi:\Lambda\to\Lambda a
,
x\mapsto xa
. To see that
\varphi
is a bimodule isomorphism, note that it is clearly surjective and is injective since
f
is a bijection.

Since
f
is an isomorphism, we have
I=\Lambda a+nI
as ideals in
\Lambda
and so there is an equality of finitely generated abelian groups
I/\Lambda a=n\cdot I/\Lambda a
. Hence, as an abelian group,
I/\Lambda a
is finite of order
m
where
(n,m)=1
. Since
m\cdot I/\Lambda a=0
, we have that
mI\subseteq\Lambda a
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.8
.

Let
I\subseteq\Lambda
be a two-sided ideal such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and which is generated by central elements. By
Corollary

2.12
, this implies that
\Lambda_{(p)}\cong I_{(p)}
are isomorphic as bimodules for all
p
. By
Lemma

3.9
, there is a
(\Lambda/n,\Lambda/n)
-bimodule isomorphism
f:\Lambda/n\to I/n
,
1\mapsto[a]
for some
a\in Z(\Lambda)\cap I
.
By
Lemma

3.10
, this implies that there is a
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodule isomorphism
\psi:\Lambda a\to\Lambda
,
xa\mapsto x
and there exists
m\neq 0
with
(n,m)=1
and
mI\subseteq\Lambda a
.
Let

J=\psi(mI)\subseteq\psi(\Lambda a)=\Lambda,

which is a two-sided ideal since
\psi
is a map of bimodules.
Finally, note that the map
I\to J
,
x\mapsto\psi(mx)
is a
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodule isomorphism, and
m=\psi(ma)\in\psi(mI)=J
implies that
J\cap\mathbb{Z}=(m_{0})
where
m_{0}\mid m
and so
(n,m_{0})=1
.
∎

We will need the following lemma. In the statement of
Lemma

3.6
, this shows (i) implies (ii).

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be as above and suppose
I\subseteq\Lambda
,
I_{k}\subseteq\Lambda_{k}
are two-sided ideals such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
,
I_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
and
(i_{k})_{\#}(I)\cong(I_{k})_{i_{k}}
are isomorphic as
(\Lambda_{k},\Lambda)
-bimodules for
k=1,2
. Then, for all
P_{k}\in\Cls(\Lambda_{k})
for
k=1,2
, there is a bijection

I\otimes_{\Lambda}-:\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\to\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(I_{1}\otimes_{\Lambda_{1}}P_{1},I_{2}\otimes_{\Lambda_{2}}P_{2}).

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.9
, there exists a two-sided ideal
J\subseteq\Lambda
such that
J\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and

I\otimes_{\Lambda}J\cong\Lambda\cong J\otimes_{\Lambda}I

as
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodules. In particular,
I
is invertible as a bimodule and determines a bijection

I\otimes_{\Lambda}-:\Cls(\Lambda)\to\Cls(\Lambda)

with inverse
J\otimes_{\Lambda}-
.

Now suppose
P\in\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})
, i.e. that
(i_{k})_{\#}(P)\cong P_{k}
are isomorphic as left
\Lambda_{k}
-modules for
k=1,2
. Then:

\displaystyle(i_{k})_{\#}(I\otimes_{\Lambda}P)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{k}\otimes_{\Lambda}(I\otimes_{\Lambda}P)\cong(I_{k})_{i_{k}}\otimes_{\Lambda}P

\displaystyle\cong(I_{k}\otimes_{\Lambda_{k}}\Lambda_{k})\otimes_{\Lambda}P\cong I_{k}\otimes_{\Lambda_{k}}P_{k}

and so

(I\otimes_{\Lambda}-)(\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2}))\subseteq\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(I_{1}\otimes_{\Lambda_{1}}P_{1},I_{2}\otimes_{\Lambda_{2}}P_{2}).

Similarly, we can show that

(J\otimes_{\Lambda}-)(\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(I_{1}\otimes_{\Lambda_{1}}P_{1},I_{2}\otimes_{\Lambda_{2}}P_{2}))\subseteq\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2}).

Hence
I\otimes_{\Lambda}-
restricts to the required bijection. ∎

Finally, we will now use
Proposition

3.8
to complete the proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

By
Lemma

3.11
, it suffices to prove part (i) only.
Let
k=1
or
2
. By
Proposition

3.8
we can assume, by replacing
I_{k}
with a bimodule isomorphic two-sided ideal, that
I_{k}\cap\mathbb{Z}
is coprime to
|\widebar{\Lambda}|
. Let
n\in I_{k}\cap\mathbb{Z}
be such that
n\neq 0
and let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
nm\equiv 1
mod
|\widebar{\Lambda}|
, which exists since
(|\widebar{\Lambda}|,n)=1
. Consider the left
\widebar{\Lambda}
-module homomorphisms

\psi_{k}:\widebar{\Lambda}\to\widebar{\Lambda}\otimes_{\Lambda_{k}}I_{k},\quad 1\mapsto m\otimes n,\quad\quad\varphi_{k}:\widebar{\Lambda}\otimes_{\Lambda_{k}}I_{k}\to\widebar{\Lambda},\quad x\otimes y\mapsto xj_{k}(y)

where
x\in\widebar{\Lambda}
and
y\in I_{k}\subseteq\Lambda_{k}
. Note that

\varphi_{k}(\psi_{k}(1))=mj_{k}(n)=mn=1\in\widebar{\Lambda}

and

\psi_{k}(\varphi_{k}(x\otimes y))=(xj_{k}(y)m)\otimes n=xm\otimes yn=xmn\otimes y=x\otimes y.

This shows that
\psi_{k}
and
\varphi_{k}
are mutual inverses and so are both bijections.

Now let

M=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in I_{1}\times I_{2}:\varphi_{1}(1\otimes x_{1})=\varphi_{2}(1\otimes x_{2})\}\subseteq\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2},

which is a left
\Lambda
-module under the action
\lambda\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})=(i_{1}(\lambda)x_{1},i_{2}(\lambda)x_{2})
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
. This coincides with the standard pullback construction for projective module over a Milnor square
\mathcal{R}

[
Mil71
]
.
However, for the
\varphi_{k}
chosen above, we further have that

M=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in I_{1}\times I_{2}:j_{1}(x_{1})=j_{2}(x_{2})\}

and so
M
is a
(\Lambda,\Lambda)
-bimodule with action

\lambda\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot\mu=(i_{1}(\lambda)\cdot x_{1}\cdot i_{1}(\mu),i_{2}(\lambda)\cdot x_{2}\cdot i_{2}(\mu))

for
\lambda
,
\mu\in\Lambda
.

Note that
M\subseteq\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}\cdot(\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2})=\mathbb{Q}\cdot\Lambda
and so there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k\neq 0
for which
kM\subseteq\Lambda
. Hence
I=kM
is a two-sided ideal in
\Lambda
which is bimodule isomorphic to
M
. Now note that
M\in\Cls(\Lambda)
as a left
\Lambda
-module by
[
RU74
, Lemma 4.4]
, and so
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
. Finally note that, by the proof of
[
Mil71
, Theorem 2.3]
, the map
f:(i_{k})_{\#}(M)\to(I_{k})_{i_{k}}
which sends
\lambda_{k}\otimes(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto\lambda_{k}\cdot x_{k}
for
\lambda_{k}\in\Lambda_{k}
is a left
\Lambda_{k}
-module isomorphism. This is also a right
\Lambda
-module isomorphism since

\displaystyle f((\lambda_{k}\otimes(x_{1},x_{2}))\cdot\lambda)

\displaystyle=f(\lambda_{k}\otimes(x_{1}\cdot i_{1}(\lambda),x_{2}\cdot i_{2}(\lambda)))

\displaystyle=\lambda_{k}\cdot x_{k}\cdot i_{k}(\lambda)=f(\lambda_{k}\otimes(x_{1},x_{2}))\cdot i_{k}(\lambda)

and so
(i_{k})_{\#}(I)\cong(i_{k})_{\#}(M)\cong(I_{k})_{i_{k}}
are bimodule isomorphic, as required.
∎

3.4.
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

By
Theorem

2.17
and
Lemma

3.4
, it suffices to show that

|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},P_{2})|=K_{\mathcal{R}}

for all
\widetilde{P}_{1}
. By
Lemma

3.4
(ii), there is a bijection
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},P_{2})\cong\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\Lambda_{2})
and, by
Lemma

3.5
, we have that
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})|=K_{\mathcal{R}}
. Hence it suffices to show that, for all
\widetilde{P}_{1}
,
there is a bijection
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\Lambda_{2})\cong\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
.

By assumption, there exists a two-sided ideal
I_{1}\subseteq\Lambda_{1}
such that
I_{1}\cong\widetilde{P}_{1}
as left
\Lambda_{1}
-modules and such that
(I_{1})_{p}\cong(\Lambda_{1})_{p}
are isomorphic as bimodules for all primes
p\mid|\widebar{\Lambda}|
. By
Lemma

3.6
, there exists a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda
such that
I\in\Cls(\Lambda)
and
(i_{1})_{\#}(I)\cong(I_{1})_{i_{1}}
as
(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda)
-bimodules and
(i_{2})_{\#}(I)\cong(\Lambda_{2})_{i_{2}}
as
(\Lambda_{2},\Lambda)
-bimodules. By
Lemma

3.11
, this induces a bijection

I\otimes_{\Lambda}-:\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})\to\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(I_{1},\Lambda_{2}),

and so there a bijections
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\widetilde{P}_{1},\Lambda_{2})\cong\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})
, as required.

4.
Application to integral group rings

The aim of this section will be to specialise
Theorem

3.1
to the case of integral group rings
\mathbb{Z}G
.
We will begin by deducing
Theorem

C
from
Theorem

3.1
. We will then prove
Theorem

D
by combining this with an additional cancellation theorem of R. G. Swan which is given in
Theorem

4.10
.

4.1.
Proof of
Theorem

C

We will begin by establishing the following. This was noted in
[
Nic21a
, Section 2]
and follows directly from
[
CR87
, Example 42.3]
.

.

where
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G/\Sigma_{N}
,
\Sigma_{N}=\sum_{g\in N}g\in\mathbb{Z}G
and
n=|N|
. Furthermore,
(G,H)
satisfies the relative Eichler condition if and only if
\Lambda
satisfies the Eichler condition.

The statement of
Theorem

C
now follows immediately from
Theorem

3.1
. In particular, the condition that
\Lambda_{2}
satisfies the Eichler condition is fulfilled since
H
is an Eichler quotient of
G
.

4.2.
Proof of
Theorem

D
for quaternionic quotients

We will now prove the following.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
G
be a finite group which has an Eichler quotient
H
of the form

Q_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20}.

Then
\mathbb{Z}G
has projective cancellation.

We will show that conditions (i), (ii) of
Theorem

C
are satisfied for
H=Q_{8}
,
Q_{12}
,
Q_{16}
or
Q_{20}
. In particular, for each of these groups, we will show:

(i)

The map
\mathbb{Z}H^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}H)
is surjective

(ii)

Every finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}H
-module is left isomorphic to an ideal in
\mathbb{Z}H
which is generated by central elements.

By
[
MOV83
, Theorems 7.15-7.18]
and the fact that
|C(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}])|=|C(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{5}])|=1
, we have that
K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}H)
is represented by units for
H\in\{Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20}\}
. Thus
\mathcal{R}_{G,H}
satisfies conditions (i).

Remark 4.3
.

It also follows from
[
MOV83
, Theorems 7.15-7.18]
that condition (i) fails for
H=Q_{116}
.

We say that a locally free module
P\in\Cls(\Lambda)
is
represented by a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\Lambda
if
P\cong I
as left
\Lambda
-modules.
It remains to prove the following, i.e. that
\mathcal{R}_{G,H}
satisfies condition (ii).

Proposition 4.4
.

For
2\leq n\leq 5
, every
P\in\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
is represented by a two-sided ideal
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
which is generated by central elements.

We begin by discussing two families of two-sided ideals which will suffice to represent all projective modules
P\in\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
in the case
2\leq n\leq 5
. Note that, by
[
Swa83
, Theorem III]
, we have that
|\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})|=2
for
2\leq n\leq 5
.

4.2.1.
Swan modules

Let
G
be a finite group, let
N=\sum_{g\in G}g
denote the group norm and let
r\in\mathbb{Z}
with
(r,|G|)=1
. The two-sided ideal
(N,r)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}G
is projective as a left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module and is known as a
Swan module
. If
r\equiv s
mod
|G|
, then
(N,r)\cong(N,s)

[
Swa60b
, Lemma 6.1]
and so we often write
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
. Note that
N,r\in Z(\mathbb{Z}G)
and so
(N,r)
is generated by central elements.

By
[
Swa83
, Theorem VI]
, we have that
[(N,3)]\neq 0\in C(\mathbb{Z}Q_{2^{n}})
for
n\geq 3
where
(N,3)
is a Swan module. Since
|\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{2^{n}})|=2
for
n=3,4
, this implies that:

\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{8})=\{\mathbb{Z}Q_{8},(N,3)\},\quad\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{16})=\{\mathbb{Z}Q_{16},(N,3)\}.

This implies
Proposition

4.4
for the groups
Q_{8}
and
Q_{16}
.

4.2.2.
Two-sided ideals of Beyl and Waller

In order to prove
Proposition

4.4
for the groups
Q_{12}
and
Q_{20}
, we will now consider a family of projective two-sided ideals in
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
which were first introduced by R. Beyl and N. Waller in
[
BW05
]
.

For
n\geq 2
, define
P_{a,b}=(a+by,1+x)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(a^{2}+b^{2},2n)=1
if
n
is odd and
(a^{2}-b^{2},2n)=1
if
n
is even. It follows from
[
BW05
, Proposition 2.1]
that
P_{a,b}
is a two-sided ideal and is projective as a left
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-module. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
, let
(\alpha)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
denote the two-sided ideal generated by
\alpha
. If
n
is odd, then there is a Milnor square

\mathcal{R}=\hbox to163.19pt{\vbox to54.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 81.59315pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{
