where
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}/(x+1)\cong\mathbb{Z}[j]
and
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}/(x^{n-1}-x^{n-2}+\cdots-1)\cong\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{2n},j]\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
. If
n=p
is an odd prime then, by Propositions
2.14
and
2.15
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{2p},j],\mathbb{Z}[j])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}([\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{2p},j]],[\mathbb{Z}[j]])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}}{\mathbb{Z}[j]^{\times}\times\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p},j]^{\times}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{K_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[j])}{K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[j])\times K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{2p},j])}}
\scriptstyle[\,\cdot\,]
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

where the vertical maps are one-to-one correspondences and
\varphi
is induced by
\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[j])
.
It follows from
[
BW05
, Proposition 2.2]
that
P_{a,b}\in\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{2n},j],\mathbb{Z}[j])
with corresponding element
[a+bj]\in\frac{\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}}{\mathbb{Z}[j]^{\times}\times\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p},j]^{\times}}
.

This allows us to deduce the following, which is an extension of
[
BW08
, Theorem 3.11]
in the case where
n=p
is an odd prime.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
p
be an odd prime with
|C(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}])|
odd and let
P_{a,b}=(a+by,1+x)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4p}
for
(a^{2}+b^{2},4p)=1
. Then:

(i)

P_{a,b}
is free if and only if
p\mid a
or
p\mid b

(ii)

P_{a,b}
is stably free if and only if
a^{2}+b^{2}
is a square mod
p
.

Proof.

By
[
MOV83
, Lemma 7.5]
, we have that
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p},j]^{\times}=\langle\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times},j\rangle
. Furthermore, the map
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{\times}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}
sends
\zeta_{p}
to
1
and so has image
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
since units of any length are achievable. This implies that:

\frac{\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}}{\mathbb{Z}[j]^{\times}\times\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p},j]^{\times}}\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\cdot\langle j\rangle

and so
P_{a,b}
is free if and only if
[a+bj]=1\in\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\cdot\langle j\rangle
.

Since
\mathbb{Z}[j]
is a Euclidean Domain, we have
K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[j])=\mathbb{Z}[j]^{\times}=\{\pm 1,\pm j\}
and, since
\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]
is a finite and hence semilocal ring, we have
K_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[j])\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}
.
It follows from
[
MOV83
, Lemmas 7.5/7.6]
that, if
|C(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}])|
odd, then
\IM(K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p},j]))=\langle\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times},\Ker(N))\rangle
where
N:\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
,
x+yj\mapsto x^{2}+y^{2}
is the norm on
\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]
. In particular, there is an isomorphism:

N:\frac{K_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[j])}{K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[j])\times K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{2p},j])}\cong\frac{\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}}{\langle\mathbb{Z}[j]^{\times},\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times},\Ker(N)\rangle}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}/N(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}/(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}.

Hence the map
\varphi
coincides by the map

N:\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\cdot\langle j\rangle\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}/(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}

which is induced by
N:\mathbb{F}_{p}[j]^{\times}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
,
x+yj\mapsto x^{2}+y^{2}
. In particular,
P_{a,b}
is stably free if and only if
[a^{2}+b^{2}]=1\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
. The result follows by evaluating these conditions.
∎

If
p=3
or
5
then, as noted above, we have that
|C(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}])|=1
. In the case
p=3
, we have
(1^{2}+2^{2},12)=1
and
3\nmid 1,2
and, in the case
p=5
, we have
(1^{2}+4^{2},20)=1
and
5\nmid 1,4
. Since
|\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4p})|=2
for
p=3,5
, we have:

\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{12})=\{\mathbb{Z}Q_{12},P_{1,2}\},\quad\Cls(\mathbb{Z}Q_{20})=\{\mathbb{Z}Q_{20},P_{1,4}\}.

We will now show that the
P_{a,b}
are generated by central elements. Our strategy will be to introduce a new family a two-sided ideals which are generated by central elements and show that the
P_{a,b}
can be expressed in this form.

If
s\in\mathbb{Z}
is odd, then define

z_{s}=(-1)^{\frac{s-1}{2}}(x^{-\frac{s-1}{2}}-x^{-\frac{s-3}{2}}+\cdots+x^{\frac{s-1}{2}}),\quad\widetilde{N}=1-x+x^{2}-\cdots-x^{2n-1}.

If
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, then define
\alpha_{s,t}=z_{s}+t\cdot\widetilde{N}y\in\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
n\geq 2
and let
r,s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
where
(s,2n)=(r,2n)=1
. Then
(\alpha_{s,t},r)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
is projective as a
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-module and
\alpha_{s,t}\in Z(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
z_{s}
,
\widetilde{N}\in Z(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
and
\widetilde{N}x=\widetilde{N}x^{-1}
. Hence we have
y\alpha_{s,t}=\alpha_{s,t}y
and

x\alpha_{s,t}=z_{s}x+t(\widetilde{N}x)y=z_{s}x+t(\widetilde{N}x^{-1})y=\alpha_{s,t}x,

which implies that
\alpha_{s,t}\in Z(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
.
Since
r\in(\alpha_{s,t},r)
and
(r,4n)=1
, we have that
(\alpha_{s,t},r)
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-module by
[
Swa60a
, Proposition 7.1]
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
n\geq 2
and let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
(a^{2}-(-1)^{n}b^{2},2n)=1
.

(i)

If
r=(a^{2}-(-1)^{n}b^{2})/\gcd(a,b)
, then there exists
a_{0},b_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
a\equiv a_{0}

\text{mod }r
,
b\equiv b_{0}

\text{mod }r
,
(a_{0},2n)=1
and
2n\mid b_{0}

(ii)

P_{a,b}=(\alpha_{s,t},r)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
where
s=a_{0}
and
t=b_{0}/2n
.

In particular,
P_{a,b}
is generated by central elements.

Proof.

Since
(r,2n)=1
, there exists
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
rx+2ny=1
. Then
a_{0}=a+rx(1-a)
and
b_{0}=2nyb
have the required properties.

Now recall that
P_{a,b}=(a+by,1+x)
. If
d=\gcd(a,b)
, then
\frac{1}{d}(a-by)\in\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
and so

r=\frac{1}{d}(a-by)\cdot(a+by)\in P_{a,b}.

In particular, since
a\equiv a_{0}

\text{mod }r
and
b\equiv b_{0}

\text{mod }r
, this implies that

P_{a,b}=(a+by,1+x,r)=(a_{0}+b_{0}y,1+x,r).

Let
s=a_{0}
and
t=b_{0}/2n
. If
e:\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}\to\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
is the function which evaluates at
x=-1
, then

e(\alpha_{s,t})=e(z_{s})+te(\widetilde{N})y=s+t(2n)y=a_{0}+b_{0}y.

Since
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}(1+x)=(1+x)\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
, this implies that
a_{0}+b_{0}y+\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}(1+x)=\alpha_{s,t}+\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}(1+x)
and so
P_{a,b}=(\alpha_{s,t},1+x,r)
.

Since
s=a_{0}
has
(s,2n)=1
, we can let
\ell\geq 1
be such that
\ell s\equiv 1

\text{mod }2n
. Similarly to the proof of
[
BW08
, Lemma 1.3]
, we now define

\bar{z}_{s}=(-x)^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(-x^{s})^{i}

so that
z_{s}\bar{z}_{s}\in 1+\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}\widetilde{N}
. Since
\bar{z}_{s}\alpha_{s,t}=\bar{z}_{s}z_{s}+\widetilde{N}\bar{z}_{s}ty
and
\widetilde{N}\in Z(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
, it follows that
\bar{z}_{s}\alpha_{s,t}\in 1+\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}\widetilde{N}
. Since
\widetilde{N}\in Z(\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n})
. We can now left-multiply both sides by
1+x
to get that
(1+x)\bar{z}_{s}\alpha_{s,t}=1+x
, where the
\widetilde{N}
term vanishes since
(1+x)\widetilde{N}=0
. Hence
1+x\in(\alpha_{s,t})
and so
P_{a,b}=(\alpha_{s,t},r)
. By
Lemma

4.6
, this implies that
P_{a,b}
is generated by central elements.
∎

By
Lemma

4.7
, this implies that
P_{1,2}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{12}
and
P_{1,4}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}Q_{20}
are generated by central elements. This completes the proof of
Proposition

4.4
, and hence completes the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Remark 4.8
.

This argument can also be used to prove
Theorem

D
in the case
H=\widetilde{T}
.

4.3.
Proof of
Theorem

D
for exceptional quotients

The following generalises
[
Swa83
, Corollary 13.5, Theorem 13.7]
which corresponds to the case
G=H
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
G
be a finite group which has an Eichler quotient
H
of the form

\widetilde{O},\quad\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\text{ for $n,m\geq 0$}.

Then
\mathbb{Z}G
has projective cancellation.

Let
\Lambda
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-order in a semisimple separable
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
which is finite-dimensional over
\mathbb{Q}
. Then we can write
A\cong A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r}\times B
, where the
A_{i}
are totally definite quaternion algebras with centres
K_{i}
and
B
satisfies the Eichler condition, i.e.
m_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}\otimes B)=0
. Let
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
be the projection of
\Lambda
onto
A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r}
. Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r},B)
denote the corresponding fibre square.

Suppose that
\Gamma_{\Lambda}\subseteq A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r}
is a maximal order. Then
\Gamma_{\Lambda}=\G_{1}\times\cdots\times\G_{n}
where
\G_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
is a maximal order for
i=1,\cdots,r
, and
\widebar{\Lambda}=\widebar{\Lambda}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widebar{\Lambda}_{r}
where
\widebar{\Lambda}_{i}
is a quotient of
\G_{i}
.

Recall that there is a finite extension
K/K_{i}
for which
A_{i}\otimes K\cong M_{n}(K)
where
n=[K:K_{i}]
. If
\varphi:A_{i}\otimes K\to M_{n}(K)
is an isomorphism, the
reduced norm
is the map

\nu_{i}:A_{i}\to K_{i}
,
\lambda\mapsto\det(\varphi(\lambda\otimes 1))
. It can be shown that
\nu
is independent of the choice of
K
and
\varphi
.
For an order
\Gamma_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
, this restricts to a map
\nu_{i}:\Gamma_{i}^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}_{K_{i}}^{\times}
.

Theorem 4.10

(
[
Swa83
, Theorem 13.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be as above and suppose that
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
is a maximal order in
A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r}
. For each
i=1,\cdots,r
and maximal
\mathcal{O}_{K_{i}}
-order
\Gamma_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
, suppose that:

(i)

\nu_{i}(\Gamma_{i}^{\times})=(\mathcal{O}_{K_{i}}^{\times})^{+}

(ii)

There is at most one prime
p
such that
(\widebar{\Lambda}_{i})_{(p)}=0
and
p
is ramified in
A_{i}
. If
p
exists, then
(\Gamma_{i})_{0}^{\times}=\Ker(\nu_{i}:\Gamma_{i}^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}_{K_{i}}^{\times})
has a subgroup of order
p+1
.

Then
\Lambda
has LFC if and only if
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
has LFC.

For a finite group
G
, we can write
\mathbb{Q}G\cong A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r}\times B
where the
A_{i}
are totally definite quaternion algebras and
B
satisfies the Eichler condition. As above, let
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}G}
is the projection of
\mathbb{Z}G
onto
A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{r}
and let
r=r(G)
denote the value of
r
in the decomposition of
\mathbb{Q}G
above.

The following was proven in
[
Swa83
, Proposition 4.11]
and
[
Swa83
, p84]
.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
G=\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{I}
, then
r(G)=1
and
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}G}
is a maximal order in
A_{\mathbb{Z}G}
. Furthermore:

(i)

A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
has centre
\mathbb{Z}
, is ramified only at
p=2
and
(\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}})_{0}^{\times}\cong\widetilde{T}

(ii)

A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
has centre
\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}]
, is finitely unramified and
(\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}})_{0}^{\times}\cong\widetilde{O}

(iii)

A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
has centre
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}(1+\sqrt{5})]
, is finitely unramified and
(\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}})_{0}^{\times}\cong\widetilde{I}
.

By
[
Swa83
, Lemmas 13.8, 13.9]
, we have that
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}]}\cong\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}^{n}\times\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}^{m}
for
n,m\geq 0
. The following is the key to being able to extending from proving that
\mathbb{Z}H
has PC where
H=\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
for some
n,m\geq 0
, to proving that
\mathbb{Z}G
has PC where
G
has an Eichler quotient
H
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
G
have an Eichler quotient
H
. Then the projection map
\mathbb{Q}G\to\mathbb{Q}H
induces an isomorphism
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}G}\cong\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}H}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{Q}G\cong A_{G}\times B_{G}
and
\mathbb{Q}H\cong A_{H}\times B_{H}
where the
A_{G}
,
A_{H}
are products of totally definite quaternion algebras and
B_{G}
,
B_{H}
satisfy the Eichler condition. By definition,
\G_{\mathbb{Z}G}
and
\G_{\mathbb{Z}H}
are the projections of
\mathbb{Z}G
and
\mathbb{Z}H
on to
A_{G}
and
A_{H}
respectively.

Since
H
is an Eichler quotient of
G
, we have
\mathbb{Q}G\cong\mathbb{Q}H\times B^{\prime}
where
B^{\prime}
satisfies the Eichler condition. Hence
A_{G}\cong A_{H}
and
B_{G}\cong B_{H}\times B^{\prime}
, and these isomorphisms commute with the inclusion maps into
\mathbb{Q}G
and
\mathbb{Q}H
. Consider the projection maps
\rho_{G}:\mathbb{Q}G\twoheadrightarrow A_{G}
and
\rho_{H}:\mathbb{Q}G\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Q}H\twoheadrightarrow A_{H}
. Then
\rho_{G}(\mathbb{Z}G)\cong\rho_{H}(\mathbb{Z}G)
since
A_{G}\cong A_{H}
are isomorphic as subrings of
\mathbb{Q}G
. Since
\rho_{G}(\mathbb{Z}G)\cong\G_{\mathbb{Z}G}
and
\rho_{H}(\mathbb{Z}G)\cong\G_{\mathbb{Z}H}
, the result follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.9
.

By Lemma
4.12
and the discussion above,
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}G}
is of the form
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}^{n}\times\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}^{m}
for some
n,m\geq 0
. In particular,
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}G}
is a maximal order whose components are maximal orders in
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
,
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
.
If
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
,
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
, then
[
Swa83
, p84]
implies that
\Gamma
has projective cancellation and
|C(\Gamma)|=1
. Hence
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}G}
has projective cancellation also, since it is the product of rings with projective cancellation.
In order to show that
\mathbb{Z}G
has projective cancellation, it suffices to show that the conditions (i), (ii) of Theorem
4.10
hold for maximal orders in
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
,
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
.

Firstly note that, if
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
,
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
and
A=A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
,
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
A_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
respectively, then
|C(\Gamma)|=1
implies that every maximal order in
A
is conjugate to
\Gamma
. In particular, it suffices to check (i), (ii) for
\Gamma
only.
To show (i) holds, note that
((\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\times})^{2}\subseteq\nu(\Gamma^{\times})\subseteq((\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\times})^{+}
where
K
is the centre of
A
. By Lemma
4.11
, we have that

K\in\{\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{8}+\zeta_{8}^{-1}),\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{5})=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{10}+\zeta_{10}^{-1})\}.

In each case, we have
C(\mathcal{O}_{K})=1
and so
(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{+}=(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{2}
by, for example,
[
Swa83
, Corollary B24]
. Hence
\nu(\Gamma^{\times})=((\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\times})^{+}
.
To show (ii) holds, there is nothing to check in the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{O}}
or
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{I}}
since
A
is finitely unramified by Lemma
4.11
. If
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}}
, then
A
is ramified only at
p=2
and
(\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{T}})_{0}^{\times}\cong\widetilde{T}
contains an element of order
p+1=3
. Hence this condition is satified regardless of whether or not
(\widebar{\Lambda}_{i})_{(p)}=0
for
R=\mathbb{Z}G
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

This argument can also be used to prove
Theorem

D
in the case
H=Q_{12}
(see also the remark in the proof of
[
Swa83
, Corollary 13.5]
).

By combining Theorems
4.2
and
4.9
, we have now completed the proof of
Theorem

D
.

4.4.
Projective cancellation for
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}

We will now prove the following result from the introduction. The proof will use that
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has SFC by Bley-Hofmann-Johnston
[
BHJ24
]
.

Theorem
1.7
.

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has
\PC
.

Proof.

Consider the Milnor square

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

which is induced from
Lemma

4.1
(i.e. it has the form
\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},\widetilde{T}}
) and the ring isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}]/\Sigma_{C_{2}}\cong(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])[C_{2}]/\Sigma_{C_{2}}\cong\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}].

The pullback square above induces a map

\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}:\Cls(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}])\to\Cls(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])\times\lx@nobreakspace\Cls(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])

with fibre
\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})
over each
(P_{1},P_{2})\in\Cls(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])\times\lx@nobreakspace\Cls(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])
.
Let

K_{\mathcal{R}}=\left|\frac{K_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widetilde{T}])}{K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])\times K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])}\right|

be the constant associated to
\mathcal{R}
.
Recall from
Section

2.1
that
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has PC if and only if
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})|=K_{\mathcal{R}}
for all
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
, and
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has SFC if and only if
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}],\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])|=K_{\mathcal{R}}
.
It follows from
[
Swa83
, Theorems I, III & VI]
that
\Cls(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])=\{\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}],(N,3)\}
, i.e. each element is represented by an ideal generated by central elements.
By
Lemma

3.6
, this implies that

\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})\cong\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}],\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])

for all
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
, and so
|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})|=|\Cls_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}],\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}])|
for all
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
.
It follows that
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has PC if and only if it has SFC.
Since
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has SFC by
[
BHJ24
]
, it must have PC.
∎

We will also establish the following:

Proposition 4.14
.

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has
\SFC
lifting.

Before turning to the proof, we will first establish the following lemma.

Lemma 4.15
.

The map
\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}]^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}])
is surjective.

A finite group
G
is
hyperelementary
if, for some prime
p
, it is of the form
A\rtimes B
where
B
is a
p
-group and
A
is a cyclic group of order prime to
p
.
The
Whitehead group
is
\Wh(G):=K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)/\pm G
. By Wall
[
Wal74
]
, we have
\Wh(G)\cong SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{r_{\mathbb{R}}(G)-r_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)}
where, for
\mathbb{F}
a field,
r_{\mathbb{F}}(G)
denotes the number of indecomposable factors in the Wedderburn decomposition for
\mathbb{F}G
(see also
[
Oli88
, p6]
).

Proof.

By hyperelementary induction
[
JM80
, Theorem 3]
,
\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}]^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}])
is surjective if and only if
\mathbb{Z}H^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}H)
is surjective for all hyperelementary subgroups
H\leq\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
. By the tables in GroupNames
[
Dok
]
, the hyperelementary subgroups of
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
are:

\mathcal{H}=\{C_{1},\,C_{2},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{6},\,C_{2}^{2},\,C_{2}\times C_{4},\,C_{2}\times C_{6},\,Q_{8},\,Q_{8}\times C_{2}\}.

The abelian groups
H\in\mathcal{H}
each have Sylow
p
-subgroups of the form
C_{p^{n}}
or
C_{p}\times C_{p^{n}}
for some
n\geq 0
and so
SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}H)=0
by
[
Oli88
, Theorem 14.2]
. Since
H
is abelian, this implies automatically that
\mathbb{Z}H^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}H)
is surjective (see, for example,
[
MOV83
, Section 1]
).
For
H=Q_{8}
, the map
\mathbb{Z}Q_{8}^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}Q_{8})
is surjective by
[
MOV83
, Theorems 7.15-7.18]
.

For
H=Q_{8}\times C_{2}
, we claim that
\Wh(Q_{8}\times C_{2})=0
. We have

\mathbb{R}[Q_{8}\times C_{2}]\cong\mathbb{R}Q_{8}^{2}\cong\mathbb{R}^{8}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\quad\mathbb{Q}[Q_{8}\times C_{2}]\cong\mathbb{Q}Q_{8}^{2}\cong\mathbb{Q}^{8}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}

and so
r_{\mathbb{R}}(Q_{8}\times C_{2})=r_{\mathbb{Q}}(Q_{8}\times C_{2})=10
.
It follows from
[
Oli88
, p17]
that
SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[Q_{8}\times C_{2}])=0
.
Hence
\Wh(Q_{8}\times C_{2})=0
by Wall’s formula, and so
\mathbb{Z}[Q_{8}\times C_{2}]^{\times}\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[Q_{8}\times C_{2}])
is surjective.
∎

Proof.

Note that
Lemma

4.15
implies that condition (i) of
Theorem

C
holds. Hence
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has SFC lifting by
[
Nic21b
, Theorem A]
(or by examining the proof of
Theorem

C
).
∎

The following question remains open.

Question 4.16
.

Does
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
have
\PC
lifting?

Part II
Group theoretic approach to cancellation properties

In this part, we will develop the group theory which, combined with the results in
Part

I
, will lead to our main classification results. This was discussed in
Section

1.2
. In
Section

5
, we introduce Eichler simple groups and minimal non-Eichler covers. In
Section

6
, we give algorithms which will facilitate their computation.

5.
Eichler simple groups and minimal non-Eichler covers

In this section, we will discuss properties of Eichler simple groups and minimal non-Eichler covers.
In
Section

5.1
, we will discuss the Eichler condition.
In
Section

5.2
, we introduce minimal non-Eichler covers and establish their basic properties.
In
Section

5.3
, we introduce Eichler simple groups and establish the Eichler Pushout Lemma (
Lemma

5.11
) and the Fundamental Lemma (which is
Lemma

1.5
in the introduction).
As usual,
G
and
H
will always denote finite groups.

5.1.
The Eichler condition

Recall from the introduction that a pair of finite groups
(G,H)
satisfies the
relative Eichler condition
if
G
has a quotient
H
with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
. This property is independent of the choice of surjection
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
since it is characterised by
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
and
m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
, which depend only on
G
and
H
.
If
G
has a quotient
H
, then
\mathbb{R}H
is a summand of
\mathbb{R}G
and so
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\geq m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
.
The following is well known (see, for example,
[
Nic21a
, Proposition 1.3]
).

Proposition 5.1
.

A finite group
G
satisfies the Eichler condition if and only if
G
has no quotient which is a binary polyhedral group.

We will now state an analogous characterisation of when a pair
(G,H)
satisfies the relative Eichler condition.
In
[
Nic20
, Section 1]
, we defined

\displaystyle\B(G)

\displaystyle=\{f:G\twoheadrightarrow Q\mid\text{$Q$ is a binary polyhedral group}\}/\sim

where, if
f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow Q_{1}
and
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow Q_{2}
for
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
binary polyhedral groups, then
f_{1}\sim f_{2}
if
\ker(f_{1})=\ker(f_{2})
are equal as subsets of
G
(which implies that
Q_{1}\cong Q_{2}
).
The following is
[
Nic21a
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
G
,
H
be finite groups and let
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
be a surjective group homomorphism. Then
(G,H)
satisfies the relative Eichler condition if and only if every
g\in\B(G)
factors through
f
, i.e.
f^{*}:\B(H)\to\B(G)
,
\varphi\mapsto\varphi\circ f
is bijective.

We will next establish formulae for
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
in terms of characters of complex representations.
This will be used in
Section

6.2
to compute
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
(see the remarks following
Algorithm

6.7
).
Let
V
be a simple
\mathbb{C}G
-module, which can be equivalently defined as an irreducible complex representation
\rho_{V}:G\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, and let
\chi_{V}:G\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
denote its character. If
\rho:G\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is given, we write
V_{\rho}
and
\chi_{\rho}
for its corresponding simple
\mathbb{C}G
-module and character.
The
Frobenius-Schur indicator
is defined as

\FS(\chi_{V})=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\chi_{V}(g^{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

It can be shown that
\FS(\chi_{V})\in\{1,0,-1\}
. We say
\rho
is
real
if
\FS(\chi_{V})=1
,
complex
if
\FS(\chi_{V})=0
and
quaternionic
if
\FS(\chi_{V})=-1
. See, for example,
[
Ser77
, Section 13.2]
for further details.

The following is standard but we were not able to locate a suitable reference in the literature, so we include a proof below.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group. Then
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
is equal to the number of
2
-dimensional irreducible complex representations
\rho:G\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
such that
\FS(\chi_{\rho})=-1
.

Proof.

By the Artin-Wedderburn theorem, there is a one to one correspondence between:

(1)

Simple components of
\mathbb{R}G
of the form
M_{n_{i}}(D_{i})
where
D_{i}\in\{\,\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}\,\}
is a real division algebra.

(2)

Simple
\mathbb{R}G
-modules
V
such that
\End_{\mathbb{R}G}(V)\cong D_{i}
and, using this isomorphism to view
V
as a
D_{i}
-module,
\dim_{D_{i}}(V)=n_{i}
.

Hence
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
is the number of simple
\mathbb{R}G
-modules
V
such that
\End_{\mathbb{R}G}(V)\cong\mathbb{H}
and
\dim_{\mathbb{H}}(V)=1
.

Let
i:\mathbb{R}G\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}G
and let
i^{*}
denote the restriction of scalars map.
By
[
Ser77
, p108 & Proposition 39]
, the simple
\mathbb{R}G
-modules
V
such that
\End_{\mathbb{R}G}(V)\cong\mathbb{H}
are precisely the
\mathbb{R}G
-modules of the form
i^{*}(V^{\prime})
where
V^{\prime}
is a simple
\mathbb{C}G
-module such that
\FS(\chi_{V^{\prime}})=-1
. Since
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(V^{\prime})=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(i^{*}(V^{\prime}))
, the condition that
\dim_{\mathbb{H}}(i^{*}(V^{\prime}))
is equivalent to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(V^{\prime})=2
. The result follows.
∎

5.2.
Minimal non-Eichler covers

A finite group
G
is a
minimal non-Eichler cover
(MNEC) of
H
if it is a non-Eichler cover and, for some quotient map
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
,
f
does not factor through a map
f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow H
where
G^{\prime}\neq G
is another non-Eichler cover of
H
. Let
\MNEC(H)
denote the class of minimal non-Eichler covers of
H
.

Proposition 5.4
.

\MNEC(C_{1})=\{\text{\,$Q_{2^{n}}$ for $n\geq 3$, $Q_{4p}$ for $p$ an odd prime, $\widetilde{T}$, $\widetilde{O}$, $\widetilde{I}$\,}\}
.

Proof.

If
G
is a non-Eichler cover of
C_{1}
, then
G
does not satisfy the Eichler condition. By
Proposition

5.1
,
G
has a quotient which is a BPG. This implies that, if
G
is minimal, then
G
is a BPG which admits no other BPG as a proper quotient.
For
n\geq 2
, we have
Q_{8n}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{2^{m}}
for some
m\geq 3
. For
n\geq 3
odd, we have
Q_{4n}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{4p}
for
p\mid n
an odd prime. Hence
G
is one of the groups listed.

Conversely, each of these groups have no proper quotients which are BPGs. To see this, note that the quotients of
Q_{4n}
are abelian or of the form
Q_{4m}
and
D_{2m}
for
m\geq 2
and
m\mid n
(see, for example, by
[
Nic21b
, Lemmas 6.5-6.6]
). For
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{I}
, this follows from GroupNames
[
Dok
]
.
∎

Note that a group
G
has a BPG quotient if and only if
G
has a quotient in
\MNEC(C_{1})
, i.e. the list of groups (i)-(iii) above. This is because, by definition, all BPGs have a quotient which is one of these groups. This generalises to the following, which was stated briefly in the introduction.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
G
be a finite group which has a quotient
H
. Then
G
is
H
-Eichler if and only if
G
has no quotient in
\MNEC(H)
.

Proof.

Suppose
G
has a quotient
G^{\prime}\in\MNEC(G)
. Since
G^{\prime}\in\MNEC(H)
,
G^{\prime}
has a non-Eichler quotient
H
, and so
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G^{\prime})>m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
. Hence
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\geq m_{\mathbb{H}}(G^{\prime})>m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
and so
G
is not
H
-Eichler.
Conversely, suppose
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
is a quotient and
G
is not
H
-Eichler. By definition,
f
factors through some
f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow H
where
G^{\prime}\in\MNEC(H)
and so
G
has a quotient in
\MNEC(H)
.
∎

For a class of finite groups
S
, define
\MNEC(S)
to be the class of groups
G
such that
G\in\MNEC(H)\setminus S
for some
H\in S
and, if
G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}\in\MNEC(H^{\prime})\setminus S
for some
H^{\prime}\in S
, then
G\cong G^{\prime}
.
We also define
\widetilde{\MNEC}(S)=\left(\bigcup_{H\in S}\MNEC(H)\right)\,\setminus\,S
. For a class of groups
S
, define the
quotient filter

S^{\QF}
to be the subclass of groups in
S
with no proper quotients in
S
.
The following is then immediate from the definition.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
S
be a class of groups. Then
\MNEC(S)=(\widetilde{\MNEC}(S))^{\QF}
.

Remark 5.7
.

(i)
The analogue of
Proposition

5.5
does not hold for arbitrary sets of groups
S
.
For example, if
S=\{C_{1},Q_{24}\}
, then
G=Q_{24}
is
S
-Eichler but has a quotient
Q_{8}\in\MNEC(S)
.

(ii) Whilst
S\cap\MNEC(S)=\emptyset
by definition, it is possible that
S\cap\MNEC^{n}(S)\neq\emptyset
for some
n\geq 2
.
For example, if
S=\{C_{1},Q_{8}\times C_{2}\}
, then
Q_{8}\times C_{2}\in S\cap\MNEC^{2}(S)
.

5.3.
Eichler simple groups

Recall from the introduction that a finite group
G
is
Eichler simple
if it has no proper Eichler quotients. We let
\mathscr{E}
denote the class of Eichler simple groups, and say a subset
S\subseteq\mathscr{E}
is
closed under quotients
if
G\twoheadrightarrow H
for
G\in S
and
H\in\mathscr{E}
implies
H\in S
.

The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 5.8
.

\mathscr{E}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
. That is, a finite group
G
is Eichler simple if and only if
G\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
for some
n\geq 0
.

This allows us to view
\mathscr{E}
as a directed graph where there is an edge from
G
to
H
if
G\in\MNEC(H)
. This approach will be the basis for computing groups in
\mathscr{E}
(see
Fig.

4
).

Before turning to the proof, we will establish a number of properties of Eichler simple groups.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
G\in\mathscr{E}
and let
H
be a finite group. Then there is a quotient
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
if and only if
G\in\MNEC^{n}(H)
for some
n\geq 0
.

Taking the graph theoretic perspective on
\mathscr{E}
, this implies that there is a quotient
G\twoheadrightarrow H
for
G,H\in\mathscr{E}
if and only if there is a directed path in
\mathscr{E}
from
G
to
H
.

Proof.

Let
G\in\mathscr{E}
. If
G\in\MNEC^{n}(H)
, then
G\twoheadrightarrow H
. Conversely, suppose
G
has a quotient
H
.
If
G\twoheadrightarrow H
is Eichler, then
G\cong H\in\MNEC^{0}(H)
since
G
is Eichler simple. If not, then
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
for some
H_{1}\in\MNEC(H)
by the definition of minimal non-Eichler cover.
If
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
is Eichler, then
G\cong H_{1}\in\MNEC(H_{1})
. If not, we can repeat this to obtain a sequence of quotients

G\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow[\mkern 4.0mu]{\mkern 4.0mu}$\cr$\rightarrow\mkern 4.0mu$\crcr}}}H_{n}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow[\mkern 4.0mu]{\MNEC\mkern 4.0mu}$\cr$\rightarrow\mkern 4.0mu$\crcr}}}\cdots\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow[\mkern 4.0mu]{\MNEC\mkern 4.0mu}$\cr$\rightarrow\mkern 4.0mu$\crcr}}}H_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow[\mkern 4.0mu]{\MNEC\mkern 4.0mu}$\cr$\rightarrow\mkern 4.0mu$\crcr}}}H_{0}=H

where, for each
0\leq i<n
, we have
H_{i+1}\in\MNEC(H_{i})
. If there exists
n\geq 0
for which
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{n}
is an Eichler quotient, then
G\cong H_{n}\in\MNEC^{n}(H)
.
If no such
n
exists, then the sequence
H_{0},H_{1},\ldots
is infinite.
Since
H_{i+1}\twoheadrightarrow H_{i}
is non-Eichler, it is proper and so
|H_{i+1}|>|H_{i}|
. In particular,
|H_{i}|\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
. This is a contradiction since
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{i}
for all
i
and so
|H_{i}|\leq|G|
for all
i
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Minimal non-Eichler covers of Eichler simple groups are Eichler simple, i.e. if
H\in\mathscr{E}
and
G\in\MNEC(H)
, then
G\in\mathscr{E}
.

The proof of
Proposition

5.10
will be an application of the following general group theoretic lemma. This will also be used later in the proof of the Fundamental Lemma (
Lemma

1.5
).

Lemma 5.11

(Eichler Pushout Lemma)
.

Let
f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
be a quotient and let
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
be an Eichler quotient. Then there exists a finite group
\overline{H}
, an Eichler quotient
\overline{f}_{1}:H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
and a quotient
\overline{f}_{2}:H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
such that
\overline{f}_{1}\circ f_{1}=\overline{f}_{2}\circ f_{2}
.
i.e. the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H}}
E
E

where
E denotes an Eichler map. Furthermore,
H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
is Eichler if and only if
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
is Eichler.

Proof.

Let
N_{1},N_{2}\leq G
be normal subgroups such that
G/N_{1}\cong H_{1}
and
G/N_{2}\cong H_{2}
. Let
\overline{H}=G/(N_{1}\cdot N_{2})
, let
\overline{f}_{1}
and
\overline{f}_{2}
be the natural quotient maps.

We now claim that
\overline{f}_{1}
is Eichler. By
Proposition

5.2
it suffices to show that every binary polyhedral quotient
f:H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow Q
(i.e. a quotient with
Q
binary polyhedral) factors through
\overline{f}_{1}
. Let
g_{1}:H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow Q
be a binary polyhedral quotient. Then
g_{1}\circ f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow Q
is a binary polyhedral quotient. Since
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
is Eichler,
Proposition

5.2
implies that
g_{1}\circ f_{1}
factors through
f_{2}
, i.e. there exists a binary polyhedral quotient
g_{2}:H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow Q
such that
g_{1}\circ f_{1}=g_{2}\circ f_{2}
.
Let
F:=g_{1}\circ f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow Q
. Then
N_{1}=\ker(f_{1})\subseteq\ker(F)
and, since
F=g_{2}\circ f_{2}
also, we have
N_{2}=\ker(f_{2})\subseteq\ker(F)
. This implies that
N_{1}\cdot N_{2}\subseteq\ker(F)
and so
F
factors through the quotient map
\overline{f}_{1}\circ f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow G/(N_{1}\cdot N_{2})=:\overline{H}
, i.e. there exists
q:\overline{H}\twoheadrightarrow Q
such that
g_{1}\circ f_{1}=q\circ\overline{f}_{1}\circ f_{1}
. Since
f_{1}
is surjective, this implies that
g_{1}=q\circ\overline{f}_{1}
. In particular,
g_{1}
factors through
\overline{f}_{1}
as required.

For the last part, note that
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H_{2})
and
m_{\mathbb{H}}(H_{1})=m_{\mathbb{H}}(\overline{H})
. Hence
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H_{1})
if and only if
m_{\mathbb{H}}(H_{2})=m_{\mathbb{H}}(\overline{H})
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.10
.

For convenience, we will use the notation of
Lemma

5.11
.
Let
H_{1}\in\mathscr{E}
and
G\in\MNEC(H_{1})
. If
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
is an Eichler quotient, then there exists an Eichler quotient
H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
and a quotient
H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
such that there is a commutative diagram of quotients as in
Lemma

5.11
. Since
H_{1}\in\mathscr{E}
, the map
H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
is an isomorphism. It follows that
G\twoheadrightarrow H
factors through the quotient
H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow H
which is non-Eichler by the last part of
Lemma

5.11
. Since
G\in\MNEC(H)
, it follows that the map
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
is an isomorphism. Hence
G\in\mathscr{E}
, as required.
∎

We can now deduce
Theorem

5.8
from Propositions
5.9
and
5.10
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.8
.

If
G\in\mathscr{E}
, then
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{1}
and so
Proposition

5.9
implies that
G\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
for some
n\geq 0
. Conversely, if
G\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
for some
n\geq 0
, then
Proposition

5.10
implies that
G\in\mathscr{E}
. Hence
\mathscr{E}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
. ∎

We will next show the following. This implies that
\mathscr{E}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
is a disjoint union and, for all
N\geq 1
, the class
S=\bigcup_{n=0}^{N}\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})\leq\mathscr{E}
is closed under quotients.

Proposition 5.12
.

(i)

In
n\neq m
, then
\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})\cap\MNEC^{m}(C_{1})=\emptyset
.

(ii)

If
G\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
and
H\in\MNEC^{m}(C_{1})
and
G\twoheadrightarrow H
, then
n>m
or
n=m
and
G\cong H
.

Proof.

We will start by proving (ii).
If
n<m
, then there is a non-Eichler quotient
H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}
for some
H^{\prime}\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})
. By composition, this implies that there is a proper quotient
G\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}
with
G,H^{\prime}\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})=\MNEC(S)
where
S=\MNEC^{n-1}(C_{1})
. This is a contradiction by the definition of
\MNEC(S)
. If
n=m
, the same argument implies that
G\cong H
.

To prove (i), suppose
G\in\MNEC^{n}(C_{1})\cap\MNEC^{m}(C_{1})
for
n\neq m
. Since
G\twoheadrightarrow G
and
n\neq m
, applying (ii) twice gives that
n>m
and
m>n
which is a contradiction.
∎

For the purposes of computations in
Part

III
, we will need to consider Eichler simple groups with no quotients in a given set.
For sets of groups
S
,
B
, define

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{B}

\displaystyle=\{G\in\mathscr{E}:\text{$G$ has no quotient in $B$}\}

\displaystyle\MNEC_{B}(S)

\displaystyle=\{G\in\MNEC(S):\text{$G$ has no quotient in $B$}\}.

It follows from
Theorem

5.8
that
\mathscr{E}_{B}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\MNEC_{B}^{n}(C_{1})
. As before, we say a subset
S\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{B}
is
closed under quotients
if
G\twoheadrightarrow H
for
G\in S
and
H\in\mathscr{E}_{B}
implies
H\in S
.

5.4.
The Fundamental Lemma

The following is the main result of this section. For
S
a specially chosen collection of Eichler simple groups, this makes it possible to determine the class of
S
-Eichler groups in terms of minimal non-Eichler covers.

Lemma
1.5

(Fundamental Lemma)
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathscr{E}
be a class of Eichler simple groups which is closed under quotients.
Then a finite group
G
is
S
-Eichler if and only if
G
has no quotient in
\MNEC(S)
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
):
Suppose
G
is
S
-Eichler and has a quotient in
\MNEC(S)
. So there is an Eichler quotient
G\twoheadrightarrow H
for
H\in S\subseteq\mathscr{E}
and a quotient
G\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}
for
H^{\prime}\in\MNEC(S)
. By
Proposition

5.10
, we have
H^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}
. By the Eichler Pushout Lemma (
Lemma

5.11
), there exists a finite group
\overline{H}
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H}}
E
E

where E denotes an Eichler map. Since
H^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}
is Eichler simple, the map
H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{H}
is an isomorphism and so there is a quotient map
H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}
. Since
H\in S
and
S
is closed under quotients, this implies that
H^{\prime}\in S
. This is a contradiction since
H^{\prime}\in\MNEC(S)
and
S\cap\MNEC(S)=\emptyset
.

(
\Leftarrow
):
Suppose
G
has no quotient in
\MNEC(S)
but is not
S
-Eichler. Let
H\in S
be a group of maximal order such that
G\twoheadrightarrow H
. Since
S
is closed under quotients, we have
C_{1}\in S
and so such a group
H
exists. Since
G
is not
S
-Eichler,
G
is not
H
-Eichler. By
Proposition

5.5
,
G
has a quotient
H^{\prime}\in\MNEC(H)
. Since
H^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow H
is a non-Eichler cover, we have that
|H^{\prime}|>|H|
and so
H^{\prime}\not\in S
since
G
has no quotients in
S
or order
>|H|
. This implies that
H^{\prime}
has a quotient in
\MNEC(S)
and so
G
has a quotient in
\MNEC(S)
, which is a contradiction.
∎

The following version for
\mathscr{E}_{B}
will is useful from the perspective of computations. This follows immediately from
Lemma

1.5
since, if
G
has no quotient in
B
, then
G
has a quotient in
\MNEC(S)
if and only if
G
has a quotient in
\MNEC_{B}(S)
.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
B\subseteq\mathscr{E}
be a subset and let
S\leq\mathscr{E}_{B}
be a class which is closed under quotients. If
G
is a finite group which has no quotients in
B
, then
G
is
S
-Eichler if and only if
G
has no quotient in
\MNEC_{B}(S)
.

6.
Algorithms for computing Eichler simple groups

We will now develop methods for computing Eichler simple groups, with a view to explicit computations which we implement in GAP
[
GAP
]
and Magma
[
BCP97
]
. In
Section

6.1
, we describe the groups in
\MNEC(H)
in terms of pullbacks. This is the basis for algorithms for computing
\MNEC(H)
given in
Section

6.2
.
As usual,
G
and
H
will always denote finite groups.

6.1.
MNECs as pullbacks

We will now prove the following. This gives us a method for computing
\MNEC(H)
for a group
H
.
Define
\widehat{\Quot}(G,H):=\Aut(H)\setminus\Quot(G,H)/\Aut(G)
where
\Aut(G)
and
\Aut(H)
act on
\Quot(G,H)
by pre-composition and post-composition respectively.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
H
be a finite group and let
G\in\MNEC(H)
.
Then there exists a binary polyhedral group
Q
, a finite group
A
and quotient maps
f_{1}:H\twoheadrightarrow A
,
f_{2}:Q\twoheadrightarrow A
such that:

G\cong H\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}Q:=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in H\times Q:f_{1}(x_{1})=f_{2}(x_{2})\in A\}.

Furthermore, the isomorphism class of
H\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}Q
only depends on the classes
\overline{f_{1}}\in\widehat{\Quot}(H,A)
and
\overline{f_{2}}\in\widehat{\Quot}(Q,A)
.

The proof of
Proposition

6.1
will consist of the following chain of lemmas.
For a finite group
G
, we say that two quotients
f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
and
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
are
disjoint
if
\ker(f_{1})\cap\ker(f_{2})=\{1\}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
G
be a finite group with disjoint quotients
f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
and
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
. Let
N_{i}=\ker(f_{i})
. Then:

(i)

f_{1}(N_{2})\unlhd H_{1}
and
f_{2}(N_{1})\unlhd H_{2}
are normal subgroups

(ii)

f_{1}(N_{2})\cong N_{2}
and
f_{2}(N_{1})\cong N_{1}
as groups

(iii)

G/(N_{1}\times N_{2})\cong H_{1}/f_{1}(N_{2})\cong H_{2}/f_{2}(N_{1})

(iv)

There is a pullback diagram of groups:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/(N_{1}\times N_{2})}

Proof.

(i) This follows since
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are surjective.

(ii) By the second isomorphism theorem, we have that

f_{1}(N_{2})=(N_{1}\cdot N_{2})/N_{1}\cong N_{2}/(N_{1}\cap N_{2})=N_{2}

where the last equality is since
N_{1}\cap N_{2}=\{1\}
. Similarly for
f_{2}(N_{1})
.

(iii)
By symmetry, it suffices to prove that
G/(N_{1}\times N_{2})\cong H_{1}/f_{1}(N_{2})
.
Since
N_{1}\cap N_{2}=\{1\}
, we have
N_{1}\times N_{2}=N_{1}\cdot N_{2}\,\unlhd\,G
.
By the third isomorphism theorem, this implies that

G/(N_{1}\times N_{2})=G/(N_{1}\cdot N_{2})\cong(G/N_{1})/((N_{1}\cdot N_{2})/N_{1})=H_{1}/f_{1}(N_{2}).

(iv) The diagram is formed by taking
N_{1}\to G
to be the inclusion map,
N_{1}\to H_{2}
to be the composition of
N_{1}\cong f_{2}(N_{1})
and inclusion, and
H_{1}\to G/(N_{1}\times N_{2})
to be the quotient map. Similarly for
N_{2}
. This is a pullback diagram by (i), (ii) and (iii).
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
G
,
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
be finite groups. Then there exists disjoint quotients
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
and
G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
if and only if there exists a group
A
and quotients
f_{1}:H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow A
,
f_{2}:H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow A
such that

G\cong H_{1}\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}H_{2}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in H_{1}\times H_{2}:f_{1}(x_{1})=f_{2}(x_{2})\in A\}.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) This follows directly from
Lemma

6.2
(iv) and the definition of pullback square of groups. In particular, take
A=G/(N_{1}\times N_{2})
with
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
the maps to
A
in the pullback square.

(
\Leftarrow
) Without loss of generality, take
G=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in H_{1}\times H_{2}:g_{1}(x_{1})=g_{2}(x_{2})\}
for a finite group
A
and quotients
g_{1}:H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow A
,
g_{2}:H_{2}\twoheadrightarrow A
.
Let
f_{1}:G\to H_{1}
,
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto x_{1}
. This is surjective since for all
x_{1}\in H_{1}
there exists
x_{2}\in H_{2}
such that
g_{2}(x_{2})=g_{1}(x_{1})
by the surjectivity of
g_{2}
. Similarly
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
,
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto x_{2}
is surjective.
Now
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are clearly disjoint since
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\ker(f_{1})\cap\ker(f_{2})
implies
x_{1}=1
and
x_{2}=1
.
∎

The following observation is straightforward but recorded here for convenience.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
f_{1}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{1}
,
f_{2}:G\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}
be disjoint quotients.
Then
f_{1}
factors through
f_{2}
if and only if
f_{2}
is not proper. In particular,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are proper if and only if
f_{1}
does not factor through
f_{2}
and
f_{2}
does not factor through
f_{1}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
H
be a finite group, let
G\in\MNEC(H)
and let
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
be a quotient map. Then there exists
g\in\B(G)
such that
f
,
g
are disjoint quotients.

Proof.

Since
f:G\to H
is a non-Eichler, it follows from
Proposition

5.2
that there exists
g\in\B(G)
which does not factor through
f
. Let
g:G\twoheadrightarrow Q
for
Q
a binary polyhedral group.
Now define
\overline{G}=G/(\ker(f)\cap\ker(g))
. This has quotients
\overline{f}:\overline{G}\twoheadrightarrow G
and
\overline{g}:\overline{G}\twoheadrightarrow Q
which are induced by
f
and
g
respectively. Furthermore,
f
and
g
factor through
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
respectively. If
\overline{f}:G\twoheadrightarrow\overline{G}
is Eichler then, by the other direction of
Proposition

5.2
,
\overline{g}
must factor through
\overline{f}
. This now implies that
g
factors through
f
, which is a contradiction.
Hence
\overline{f}
is non-Eichler and so
\overline{G}
is another non-Eichler cover of
H
. Since
G
is a minimal non-Eichler cover, it follows that the map
G\twoheadrightarrow\overline{G}
is an isomorphism. This implies that
\ker(f)\cap\ker(g)=\{1\}
and so
f
,
g
are disjoint quotients.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2},A
be finite groups. Suppose
f_{1},f_{1}^{\prime}\in\Quot(H_{1},A)
and
f_{2},f_{2}^{\prime}\in\Quot(H_{2},A)
.

(i)

If
[f_{1}]=[f_{1}^{\prime}]\in\Quot(H_{1},A)/\Aut(H_{1})
and
[f_{2}]=[f_{2}^{\prime}]\in\Quot(H_{2},A)/\Aut(H_{2})
, then

H_{1}\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}H_{2}\cong H_{1}\times_{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime}}H_{2}
.

(ii)

If
\alpha\in\Aut(A)
, then
H_{1}\times_{\alpha\circ f_{1},f_{2}}H_{2}\cong H_{1}\times_{f_{1},\alpha^{-1}\circ f_{2}}H_{2}
.

Proof.

(i) For
i=1,2
, let
f_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i}\circ\theta_{i}
for
\theta_{i}\in\Aut(H_{i})
. Then consider the map

F:H_{1}\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}H_{2}\to H_{1}\times_{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime}}H_{2},\quad(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(\theta_{1}(x_{1}),\theta_{2}(x_{2})).

This is well defined since, if
(x_{1},x_{2})\in H_{1}\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}H_{2}
, then
f_{1}(x_{1})=f_{2}(x_{2})
and so
f_{1}^{\prime}(\theta_{1}(x_{1}))=f_{2}^{\prime}(\theta_{2}(x_{2}))
. This implies that
(\theta_{1}(x_{1}),\theta_{2}(x_{2}))\in H_{1}\times_{f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime}}H_{2}
. Then check that
F
is an isomorphism.

(ii) For all
x_{1}\in H_{1}
and
x_{2}\in H_{2}
,
\alpha(f_{1}(x_{1}))=f_{2}(x_{2})
if and only if
f_{1}(x_{1})=\alpha^{-1}(f_{2}(x_{2}))
. It follows that
H_{1}\times_{\alpha\circ f_{1},f_{2}}H_{2}
and
H_{1}\times_{f_{1},\alpha^{-1}\circ f_{2}}H_{2}
coincide as sets, and so are isomorphic.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

Let
H
be a finite group and let
G\in\MNEC(H)
. Then there is a quotient
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
. By
Lemma

6.5
, there exists a binary polyhedral group
Q
and a quotient
g:G\twoheadrightarrow Q
such that
f
and
g
are disjoint. By
Lemma

6.3
, this implies that
G\cong H\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}Q
for some quotients
f_{1}:H\twoheadrightarrow A
and
f_{2}:Q\twoheadrightarrow A
, as required. The last part follows directly from
Lemma

6.6
.
∎

6.2.
Algorithms

We will now give our main algorithm which computes
\MNEC_{B}(H)
for a given group
H
and a specially determined class of groups
B
.
The algorithm takes as input the normal subgroups and quotients of the groups
Q_{8}
,
Q_{12}
,
Q_{16}
,
Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
and
\widetilde{I}
. For convenience later on, we list them in
Fig.

2
. This follows, for example, from the tables in GroupNames
[
Dok
]
.

Group

Normal subgroups

Quotients

Q_{8}

C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{4}
,
Q_{8}

C_{1}
,

,
C_{2}^{2}
,
Q_{8}

,

,

,

,

,

C_{1}
,
