,
D_{6}
,
S_{4}
,
\widetilde{O}

\widetilde{I}

C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}

C_{1}
,
A_{5}
,
\widetilde{I}

Figure 2.
Normal subgroups and quotients of small binary polyhedral groups

Let
B_{\BPG}=\{Q_{36},Q_{60},Q_{100}\}\sqcup\{Q_{4p}:\text{$p\geq 7$}\}\sqcup\{Q_{8p}:\text{$p\geq 3$}\}\sqcup\{Q_{16p}:\text{$p\geq 3$}\}\sqcup\{Q_{2^{n}}:n\geq 4\}
, where
p
is prime and
n
is an integer.
This is the smallest class of groups for which a finite group
G
has a quotient in
B_{\BPG}
if and only if
G
has a quotient of the form
Q_{4n}
for some
n\geq 6
. Since
Q_{4n}\in\mathscr{E}
for all
n\geq 2
, we have
B_{\BPG}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
.
Let
B
be such that
B_{\BPG}\subseteq B\subseteq\mathscr{E}
.

Algorithm 6.7
.

Input a finite group
H
and a class
B
such that
B_{\BPG}\subseteq B\subseteq\mathscr{E}
.
Output
\MNEC_{B}(H)
.
Define

\BPG=\{Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20},\widetilde{T},\widetilde{O},\widetilde{I}\},\quad\BPG^{N}=\{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},C_{5},C_{6},C_{8},Q_{8},C_{10},\widetilde{T}\}.

By
Fig.

2
,
\BPG\cup\BPG^{N}
(resp.
\BPG^{N}
) is the set of normal subgroups (resp. proper normal subgroups) of the groups in
\BPG
.
Note that
\Quot(Q)
for each
Q\in\BPG
has been computed already in
Fig.

2
.
The steps are as follows:

(i)

For each
N\in\BPG^{N}
, compute the extensions of
H
by
N
, i.e. the groups
G
such that there is an extension
1\to N\to G\to H\to 1
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{N}
denote this class of groups. For each
N\in\BPG\setminus\BPG^{N}=\{Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20},\widetilde{O},\widetilde{I}\}
, let
\mathcal{S}_{N}=\{H\times N\}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{N}\mathcal{S}_{N}
where
N\in\BPG\cup\BPG^{N}
.
Compute
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
for
G\in\mathcal{S}\sqcup\{H\}
using
Proposition

5.3
.
Remove all groups
G\in\mathcal{S}
such that
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
.
Then remove all groups which have others as proper quotients. Then remove all groups which have quotients in
B
. This gives a class of groups
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
which coincides with
\MNEC_{B}(H)
.

Remark 6.8
.

We lose no generality in assuming that
B\supseteq\{Q_{4n}:n\geq 6\}
rather than
B\supseteq B_{\BPG}
. However, this does make a slight difference to the speed of the algorithm in practice.

Proof of correctness.

We claim that the algorithm terminates and has output
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\MNEC_{B}(H)
. The algorithm terminates since, for fixed
H
and
N
a soluble group, there is a finite time algorithm for determining the groups
G
such that there is an extension
1\to N\to G\to H\to 1
using group cohomology.
The case where
N
is soluble suffices since all the groups in
\BPG^{N}
are soluble (see GroupNames
[
Dok
]
).
We use
Proposition

5.3
to compute
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
. This amounts to computing the character table of
G
and the Frobenius-Schur indicators of each character, both of which can be done in finite time.

We now claim that the algorithm computes
\MNEC_{B}(H)
, i.e. that
\MNEC_{B}(H)=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
In order to prove this, let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
be the subset consisting of those
G\in\mathcal{S}
such that
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)>m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
and which have no quotients in
B
.
So we have
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
.
The order of removing groups from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
with others as proper quotients and removing those with quotients in
B
does not matter, and so

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
by removing groups with proper quotients.

We will start by showing that
\MNEC_{B}(H)\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
G\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
. Then there is a quotient
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
and, by
Lemma

6.5
, there is a quotient
f:G\twoheadrightarrow Q
for some binary polyhedral group
Q
such that
f
and
g
are disjoint. Since
G
has no quotients in
B
and so no quotients of the form
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
, we must have
Q\in\BPG
.
By
Lemma

6.2
(iv), this implies that there is an extension of groups

1\to N\to G\to H\to 1

where
N\leq Q
is a normal subgroup. In particular,
N\in\BPG\cup\BPG^{N}
. If
N\in\BPG\setminus\BPG^{N}
, then we must have
N\cong Q
and so
Lemma

6.2
(iii) implies that
G\cong H\times N
. It follows that
G\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
and so
G\in\mathcal{S}
. Since
G\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
, we have
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)>m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
and
G
has no quotients in
B
. Hence
G\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
.

We will next show that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\MNEC_{B}(H)
. Let
G\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, and so
G\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
for some
N\in\BPG\cup\BPG^{N}
. This implies that there is an extension

1\to N\to G\to H\to 1
.
Since
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)>m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
and
G
has no quotients in
B
, it follows that
G
is a non-Eichler cover of
H
. Then
G
has a quotient
G^{\prime}\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
. Since
\MNEC_{B}(H)\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
, we have
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
G\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, this implies that
G\cong G^{\prime}
and so
G\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
.

Finally, we will complete the proof by showing that
\MNEC_{B}(H)\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Let
G\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
. Then
G\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
G
is finite, we can keep quotienting until we arrive at a group with no proper quotients in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
.
That is,
G
has a quotient
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Then
G^{\prime}\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
. Since
G
is a minimal non-Eichler cover, we have
G\cong G^{\prime}
and so
G\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
∎

We will now discuss the implementation of
Algorithm

6.7
in Magma and GAP.

(i)

In order to determine the extensions of
H
by
N
when
N\in\BPG^{N}
(which is a soluble group), we use the Magma function
ExtensionsOfSolubleGroup
. We then filter the groups up to isomorphism using
IsIsomorphic
. Note that we could not do this for all
N\in\BPG\cup\BPG^{N}
since
\widetilde{I}
is not soluble.

(ii)

We compute
\mathcal{S}
by filtering up to isomorphism once more. We compute
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
for
G\in\mathcal{S}\sqcup\{H\}
as in

Proposition

5.3
. We compute the characters
\chi_{V}:G\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of the irreducible complex representations using the GAP functions
CharacterTable
and
Irr
. We then compute the degree and Frobenius-Schur indicator of each representation using the GAP functions
Degree
and
Indicator
.
Using this, we filter out groups which are not non-Eichler covers. We filter out groups which have others as proper quotients or have quotients in
B
using the function
Homomorphisms
which can be used to determine when surjective homomorphisms exist.

We obtain the following more general algorithm as an immediate consequence.

Algorithm 6.9
.

Input
S\leq\mathscr{E}
a finite closed subgraph and a class
B
such that
B_{\BPG}\subseteq B\subseteq\mathscr{E}
.
Output
\MNEC_{B}(S)
.
The steps are as follows:

(i)

For each
G\in S
, compute
\MNEC_{B}(G)
using
Algorithm

6.7
.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{G\in S}\MNEC_{B}(G)
. Remove all groups in
S
. Remove all groups which have others as proper quotients. This gives a class of groups
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
which coincides with
\MNEC_{B}(S)
.

Proof of correctness.

This follows immediately from
Proposition

5.6
and the correctness of
Algorithm

6.7
. Note that the algorithm terminates because
S
is finite.
∎

Observe that
Algorithm

6.9
terminates since
S
is finite.
When
S
is infinite, for example
S=\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}:n,m\geq 0\}
,
Algorithm

6.9
does not terminate. We discuss this case in
Section

7
.

Part III
Explicit computations

The aim of this part is to do the explicit computations which feed into our main classification results.
In
Section

7
, we will compute
\MNEC_{B}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
.
We will then use this in
Section

8
, combined with computer calculations, to obtain our main results.

7.
MNECs for the infinite family
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}

The aim of this section will be to compute
\MNEC_{B}
for the infinite family
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
.
Throughout this section we will fix
B=\{Q_{4n}:n\geq 6\}
so that all groups in
B
fail
\SFC
.

Let
C_{3}=\langle t\mid t^{3}\rangle
and
Q_{8}=\langle x,y\mid x^{2}y^{-2},yxy^{-1}x\rangle
and note that
\widetilde{T}\cong Q_{8}\rtimes_{\varphi}C_{3}
where
\varphi(t)=\theta\in\Aut(Q_{8})
and
\theta:Q_{8}\to Q_{8}
,
x\mapsto y
,
y\mapsto xy
is an automorphism of order three (see, for example, GroupNames
[
Dok
]
). By taking this as our definition for
\widetilde{T}
, we obtain a canonical quotient map
f:\widetilde{T}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}
.
For
1\leq k\leq n
, define
f_{k}=(f,\cdots,f,0,\cdots,0):\widetilde{T}^{n}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}
where there are
k
copies of
f
.
Define

Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k)}\widetilde{T}^{n}:=Q_{8}\rtimes_{i\circ f_{k}}\widetilde{T}^{n}

where
i:C_{3}\to\Aut(Q_{8})
,
t\mapsto\theta
.
We have
Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k)}\widetilde{T}^{n}\cong\widetilde{T}^{n-k}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k)}\widetilde{T}^{k})
.
For brevity, we write
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}:=Q_{8}\rtimes_{(n)}\widetilde{T}^{n}
.
The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
with
(n,m)\neq(0,0)
. Then

\MNEC_{B}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})=\{\,\widetilde{T}^{n+1}\times\widetilde{I}^{m},\,\,\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m+1},\,\,\underbrace{\widetilde{T}^{n-k}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})}_{k=1,\cdots,n},\,\,\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times C_{2}\}.

Hence
\MNEC_{B}(\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m})=\{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}\}\sqcup\{Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}:k\geq 1\}
where
n,m
ranges across the values
n,m\geq 0
,
(n,m)\neq(0,0)
.

The proof will involve a mixture of two approaches. The first is based on classifying group extensions using group cohomology. This was used to calculate
\MNEC_{B}
in Algorithm
6.7
, and is the basis of the Magma function
ExtensionsOfSolubleGroup
which we used in our implementation.
The second is based on pullbacks of group quotients using
Proposition

6.1
.

In
Section

7.1
, we dicuss group cohomology and group extensions. In
Section

7.2
, we compute
\widehat{\Quot}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m},A)
for various finite groups
A
, which is needed for the pullback approach. In
Section

7.4
, we combine the two approaches to completely determine
\widetilde{\MNEC}_{B}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
. We then use the results on the non-existence of quotients from
Section

7.3
to obtain
\MNEC_{B}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
.

7.1.
Cohomology and group extensions

The following can be found in Eilenberg-Maclane
[
EM47
]
.
Following the notation of
[
Bro82
, Section IV.6]
, suppose we have an extension

1\to N\to G\to H\to 1.

Since
N\,\unlhd\,G
, there is a homomorphism
\varphi:G\to\Aut(N)
given by conjugation. Let
\Inn(N)
denote the set of inner automorphisms of
N
and
\Out(N)=\Aut(N)/\Inn(N)
the outer automorphism group. Since
\varphi(N)=\Inn(N)
,
\varphi
induces a map
\psi:H\to\Out(N)
.
If the extension is split,
\psi
lifts to a map
\bar{\psi}:H\to\Aut(N)
. Let
\mathcal{E}(H,N)
denote the equivalence class of extensions of the above form and let
\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)
denote the those extensions in
\mathcal{E}(H,N)
which give rise to
\psi
.

In
[
EM47
]
, Eilenberg-Maclane showed that
\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)
can be computed in terms of group cohomology as follows. First note that, if
Z(N)
is the centre of
N
, then the restriction map
\Res:\Aut(N)\to\Aut(Z(N))
has
\Res(\Inn(N))=\{\id_{Z(N)}\}
and so induces a map
\Res:\Out(N)\to\Aut(Z(N))
. Hence if
\psi:H\to\Out(N)
, then the composition

\bar{\psi}=\Res\circ\psi:H\to\Aut(Z(N))

induces a
\mathbb{Z}H
-module structure on
Z(N)
which we denote by
Z(N)_{\psi}
. The following can be found in
[
Bro82
, Theorems 6.6, 6.7]
.
We often refer to (i) and (ii) as the primary and secondary obstructions for the extensions
\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
H
and
N
be groups, let
\psi:H\to\Out(N)
be a map and let
Z(N)_{\psi}
denote the centre of
N
which is a
\mathbb{Z}H
-module via
\bar{\psi}
. Then

(i)

There is an obstruction in
H^{3}(H,Z(N)_{\psi})
which vanishes if and only if
\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)\neq\emptyset

(ii)

If
\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)
is non-empty, then there is a bijection

\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)\cong H^{2}(H,Z(N)_{\psi}).

This result takes the following simpler form when
N
is abelian (see
[
Bro82
, Section IV.3]
).

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
H
and
N
be groups with
N
abelian, let
\psi:H\to\Aut(N)
be a map and let
N_{\psi}
denote the centre of
N
which is a
\mathbb{Z}H
-module via
\bar{\psi}
. Then there is a bijection

\mathcal{E}(H,N,\psi)\cong H^{2}(H,N_{\psi}).

The following will be used to calculate
\MNEC_{B}
in the following section.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
. Then

H^{2}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m};\mathbb{F}_{2})=0.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{T}\cong\SL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
and
\widetilde{I}\cong\SL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{5})
, we can use
[
AM04
, Corollary 6.18]
to deduce that
H^{*}(\widetilde{T};\mathbb{F}_{2})=H^{*}(\widetilde{I};\mathbb{F}_{2})=0
for
*=1,2
. By the Künneth formula for group cohomology
[
Wei94
, Exercise 6.1.10]
, we have that
H^{*}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m};\mathbb{F}_{2})=0
for
\ast=1,2
.
∎

7.2.
Classifying surjective group homomorphisms

Motivated by
Proposition

6.1
, we now calculate
\widehat{\Quot}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m},A)
for certain finite groups
A
.
Let
\BPG=\{Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20},\widetilde{T},\widetilde{O},\widetilde{I}\}
.
By
Fig.

2
, the non-trivial proper quotients of the groups in
\BPG
are

\BPG^{*}=\{C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},C_{2}^{2},D_{6},D_{8},D_{10},A_{4},S_{4},A_{5}\}.

Recall from
Section

6.1
that, for groups
G
and
H
, we let
\Quot(G,H)
denote the set of quotient maps
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
and
\Quot(G)
denote the set of quotients of
G
, i.e.
\{H:\Quot(G,H)\neq\emptyset\}
.
We also let
\widehat{\Quot}(G,H):=\Aut(H)\setminus\Quot(G,H)/\Aut(G)
.

Let
q:Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}^{k}
denote the standard surjection induced by the semidirect product. As before, let
f:\widetilde{T}=Q_{8}\rtimes C_{3}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}
and let
f_{r}=(f,\cdots,f,0,\cdots,0):\widetilde{T}^{k}\to C_{3}
where
0\leq r\leq k
and there are
r
copies of
f
.
Similarly, let
g:\widetilde{T}=Q_{8}\rtimes C_{3}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}\rtimes C_{3}\cong A_{4}
and let
g_{r}=(g,\cdots,g,0,\cdots,0):\widetilde{T}^{k}\to A_{4}
where
0\leq r\leq k
. Finally, let
h:\widetilde{I}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{5})\twoheadrightarrow\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{5})\cong A_{5}
and
h_{r}=(h,\cdots,h,0,\cdots,0):\widetilde{I}^{m}\to A_{5}
where
0\leq r\leq m
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
. Then

\Quot(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})\cap\BPG^{*}\subseteq\{C_{3},A_{4},A_{5}\}
. Furthermore:

(i)

If
n\geq 1
, then

\widehat{\Quot}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m},C_{3})=\{(f_{r},0):1\leq r\leq n\}
.

(ii)

If
m\geq 1
, then

\widehat{\Quot}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m},A_{5})=\{(0,h_{t}):1\leq t\leq m\}
.

We will begin by establishing the following useful lemma. The case
k=0
will be used in the proof of
Proposition

7.5
, and the general case will be used later in the proof of
Theorem

7.1
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
n,m,k\geq 0
. Then
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptC_{2}
.

Proof.

Note that, if
G
and
H
are finite groups with no
C_{2}
quotients, then
G\times H
has no
C_{2}
quotients. This follows from
the fact that
G
has a
C_{2}
quotient if and only if the abelianisation
G^{\text{ab}}
has even order, and
(G\times H)^{\text{ab}}\cong G^{\text{ab}}\times H^{\text{ab}}
.
Since
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{I}
have no
C_{2}
quotients (by
Fig.

2
),
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
has no
C_{2}
quotients.
It remains to show that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
has no
C_{2}
quotients for
k\geq 1
.

We will identify
Q_{8}=\langle x,y\mid yxy^{-1}=x^{-1}\rangle
. Recall that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
has action
\varphi:\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}\leq\Aut(Q_{8})
, where the image is generated by
\theta:x\mapsto y
,
y\mapsto xy
.
Suppose that
f:Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}\to C_{2}
is a quotient map. Since
\widetilde{T}^{k}
has no
C_{2}
quotients,
f\mid_{\widetilde{T}^{k}}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\to C_{2}
is trivial. Since
f
is non-trivial, this implies that
f\mid_{Q_{8}}:Q_{8}\to C_{2}
is a quotient map. We can act by automorphisms of
Q_{8}
, which extend to
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
, to assume that
\ker(f\mid_{Q_{8}})=\langle y\rangle
. This gives
\ker(f)=\langle y,\widetilde{T}^{k}\rangle
. Let
z\in\widetilde{T}^{k}
be such that
\varphi(z)=\theta
. Then
zyz^{-1}=\theta(y)=xy
and so
x=zyz^{-1}y^{-1}\in\ker(f)
. This implies that
\ker(f)=\langle x,y,\widetilde{T}^{n}\rangle=Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
, which
is a contradiction since we assumed
f
was non-trivial.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

7.5
.

By
Lemma

7.6
,
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
has no
C_{2}
quotient. The first part now follows since
\{C_{3},A_{4},A_{5}\}
are precisely the groups in
\BPG^{*}
with no
C_{2}
quotients.

(i) From now on, let
G=\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
.
Let
f:G\to C_{3}
be a quotient map. Then, for each copy of
\widetilde{I}
, we have
f\mid_{\widetilde{I}}:\widetilde{I}\to C_{3}
which is trivial since
\widetilde{I}
has no
C_{3}
quotients (by
Fig.

2
).
It therefore suffices to work in the case
m=0
. For each copy of
\widetilde{T}
, we have
f\mid_{\widetilde{T}}:\widetilde{T}\to C_{3}
.
By GroupNames
[
Dok
]
, we can deduce that

\Hom(\widetilde{T},C_{3})=\{1,\pm f\}
.
Since
\widetilde{T}\leq\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
, the involution on
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
induces an automorphism which maps
f\mapsto-f
. Since automorphisms can also permute the copies of
\widetilde{T}^{n}
, we can act by
\Aut(\widetilde{T}^{n})\leq\Aut(G)
to get that
f\mid_{\widetilde{T}^{n}}=f_{k}
for some
k
.
Hence
f=(f_{r},0)
for some
0\leq r\leq n
,
0\leq s\leq k
. Since
f
is surjective, we have
(r,s)\neq(0,0)
.

(ii) Let
f:G\to A_{5}
be a quotient map. For each copy of
\widetilde{I}
, we have
f\mid_{\widetilde{I}}:\widetilde{I}\to A_{5}
. By GroupNames
[
Dok
]
, this is trivial or the unique quotient map
h
. By acting by
\Aut(\widetilde{I}^{m})\leq\Aut(G)
, we can therefore assume that
f\mid_{\widetilde{I}^{m}}=h_{t}
for some
t
. For each copy of
\widetilde{T}
, we have
f\mid_{\widetilde{T}}:\widetilde{T}\to A_{5}
. This is trivial since
A_{5}
is simple and
\widetilde{T}
has no quotient
A_{5}
(by
Fig.

2
). This implies that
f=(0,h_{t})
for
0\leq t\leq m
. Since
f
is surjective, we have
t\neq 0
.
∎

7.3.
Non-existence of quotients

We now show that non-trivial quotients do not exist between certain groups in
\widetilde{\MNEC}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
.
We will use the following version of Goursat’s lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be groups. If
H_{1}\leq G_{1}
,
H_{2}\leq G_{2}
are subgroups,
N_{1}\unlhd H_{1}
,
N_{2}\unlhd H_{2}
are normal subgroups and
\varphi:H_{1}/N_{1}\to H_{2}/N_{2}
is an isomorphism, then we can define a subgroup

N_{\varphi}=\{(x,y)\in H_{1}\times H_{2}:\varphi(xN_{1})=yN_{2}\}\leq G_{1}\times G_{2}.

Every subgroup of
G_{1}\times G_{2}
is of the form
N_{\varphi}
for some
H_{1},H_{2},N_{1},N_{2}
and
\varphi
.
Furthermore, if
N_{\varphi}
is a normal subgroup, then
H_{1}\unlhd G_{1}
and
H_{2}\unlhd G_{2}
are normal.

Proof.

The main part can be found in
[
Hal18
, Theorem 5.5.1]
. Suppose
N_{\varphi}
is a normal subgroup. For
i=1,2
,
H_{i}=p_{i}(N_{\varphi})
where
p_{i}:G_{1}\times G_{2}\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}
is projection map. This implies that
H_{i}\unlhd G_{i}
is a normal subgroup since its is the image of a normal subgroup under a surjective homomorphism.
∎

We will begin by considering the quotients of
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
n\geq 1
. If
N\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n}
is a normal subgroup and
|N|=2^{a}3^{b}
, then
a\geq 3b
.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction on
n
. It is true for
n=1
since, by GroupNames
[
Dok
]
, the normal subgroups of
\widetilde{T}
have orders
1
,
2
,
8
and
24=2^{3}\cdot 3
. Suppose it is true for all
k<n
and let
N\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n}
be a normal subgroup. By Goursat’s Lemma (
Lemma

7.7
), we have that
N\cong N_{\varphi}
where
\varphi:H_{1}/N_{1}\to H_{2}/N_{2}=:A
is an isomorphism and
N_{1}\unlhd H_{1}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n-1}
,
N_{2}\unlhd H_{2}\unlhd\widetilde{T}
are normal subgroups.
It follows that
|N_{\varphi}|=|H_{1}||N_{2}|
.
By the inductive hypothesis, we know that
|H_{1}|=2^{a}3^{b}
where
a\geq 3b
.
By
Fig.

2
, we must have
H_{2}\in\{1,C_{2},Q_{8},\widetilde{T}\}
and so
N_{2}\in\{1,C_{2},C_{4},Q_{8},\widetilde{T}\}
and
|N_{2}|\in\{1,2,4,8,24\}
.
It follows that
|N_{\varphi}|=2^{a^{\prime}}3^{b^{\prime}}
where
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=(a+d,b)
for
d\in\{0,1,2,3\}
or
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=(a+3,b+1)
. In each case,
a\geq 3b
implies that
a^{\prime}\geq 3b^{\prime}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
and
k\geq 1
. Then
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
.

Proof.

Suppose
f:\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
is a quotient map. Then the image
f(\widetilde{I})
of each copy of
\widetilde{I}
is isomorphic to
1
,
A_{5}
or
\widetilde{I}
(by
Fig.

2
). Since
5\mid|A_{5}|,|\widetilde{I}|
and
5\nmid|Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}|
,
f(\widetilde{I})\not\cong A_{5}
or
\widetilde{I}
and so we must have
f(\widetilde{I})=1
for all copies of
\widetilde{I}
. It therefore suffices to consider the case where
m=0
, so that
f:\widetilde{T}^{n}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
and so we necessarily have that
n\geq k+1
by the orders of the groups.
Then
N=\ker(f)
is a normal subgroup of
\widetilde{T}^{n}
with
|N|=\frac{1}{8}|\widetilde{T}|^{n-k}=2^{3(n-k)-3}3^{n-k}
. This contradicts
Lemma

7.8
.
∎

We will now move on to the larger family
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
k\geq 1
. If
N\unlhd Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
is a normal subgroup and
|N|=2^{a}3^{b}>1
, then
a\neq 3b
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
has a normal subgroup
N
of order
2^{3t}3^{t}
where
t\geq 1
. Analogously to Goursat’s Lemma (
Lemma

7.7
), let
N_{1}=N\cap Q_{8}
and let
H_{2}
denote the projection of
N
to
\widetilde{T}^{k}
. Then
N_{1}\unlhd Q_{8}
,
H_{2}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{k}
are normal subgroups. For each
y\in H_{2}
, consider
S_{y}=\{x\in Q_{8}:(x,y)\in N\}
where
(x,y)\in Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
. Then
S_{y}=N_{1}x
for any
x\in S_{y}
and so
|N|=|N_{1}||H_{2}|
. Since
N_{1}\unlhd Q_{8}
, we have
|N_{1}|\in\{1,2,4,8\}
. Since
H_{2}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{k}
,
Lemma

7.8
implies that
|H_{2}|=2^{a}3^{b}
for
a\geq 3b
. Since
|N|=2^{3t}3^{t}
, this now implies that
|N_{1}|=1
and
|N|=|H_{2}|
. In particular, the projection map
p_{2}:Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}^{k}
restricts to an isomorphism
p_{2}\mid_{N}:N\to H_{2}
.

Now suppose
(x,y)\in N
. Since
N
is normal, we have that
(z,1)(x,y)(z^{-1},1)\in N
for all
z\in Q_{8}
. We have
(z,1)(x,y)(z^{-1},1)=(zx\varphi_{y}(z^{-1}),y)\in N
where
\varphi_{(\cdot)}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}\leq\Aut(Q_{8})
is the action defined by the semidirect product.
Since
p_{2}\mid_{N}
is an isomorphism, this implies that
x=zx\varphi_{y}(z^{-1})
and so
\varphi_{y}(z)=x^{-1}zx
for all
z\in Q_{8}
and so
\varphi_{y}\in\Inn(Q_{8})
. Since
\Inn(Q_{8})\cong Q_{8}/Z(Q_{8})\cong C_{2}^{2}
and
|\IM(\varphi_{(\cdot)})|=3
, we have that
\IM(\varphi_{(\cdot)})\cap\Inn(Q_{8})=\{\id\}
. It follows that
\varphi_{y}=\id
and so
y\in\ker(\varphi_{(\cdot)}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\to\Aut(Q_{8}))
. In particular, we have

N\cong H_{2}\unlhd\ker(\varphi_{(\cdot)}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\to\Aut(Q_{8}))\cong\ker(f_{k}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3})

where
f_{k}=(f,\cdots,f)
is as defined at the start of this section. Projection onto the first
k-1
coordinates gives a split surjection
\ker(f_{k})\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}^{k-1}
with kernel isomorphic to
Q_{8}
. The action of
\widetilde{T}^{k-1}
on
Q_{8}
is given by
\varphi^{\prime}=(i\circ f_{k-1}):\widetilde{T}^{k-1}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}\leq\Aut(Q_{8})
. That is,
\ker(f_{k}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3})\cong Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k-1}
. Hence we have a normal subgroup
H_{2}\unlhd Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k-1}
of order
2^{3t}3^{t}
.
We can repeat this argument
k
times to get that there is a normal subgroup
N^{\prime}\unlhd Q_{8}
of order
2^{3t}3^{t}
. This is a contradiction since
3\nmid|Q_{8}|
. ∎

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
and
k,r\geq 1
with
k\neq r
. Then
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{r}
.

Proof.

By the same argument as given for
Proposition

7.9
,
we can restrict to the case where
m=0
and so there is a quotient map

f:Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k)}\widetilde{T}^{n+k}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{r}

where
Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k)}\widetilde{T}^{n+k}\cong\widetilde{T}^{n}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})
. This implies that
Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k)}\widetilde{T}^{n+k}
has a normal subgroup
N
of order
2^{3t}3^{t}
where
t=n+k-r\geq 1
.
Let
N_{1}=N\cap Q_{8}
and let
H_{2}
denote the projection of
N
to
\widetilde{T}^{n+k}
, so that
N_{1}\unlhd Q_{8}
,
H_{2}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n+k}
are normal subgroups.
By an identical argument to the one given in
Lemma

7.10
, we get that

N\cong H_{2}\unlhd\ker(\varphi_{(\cdot)}:\widetilde{T}^{n+k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3})\cong\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\ker(f_{k}:\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3})\cong Q_{8}\rtimes_{(k-1)}\widetilde{T}^{n+k-1}.

We can repeat this argument
k
times to get that
N
projects to a normal subgroup
N^{\prime}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n}\times Q_{8}
of order
2^{3t}3^{t}
. By Goursat’s Lemma (
Lemma

7.7
), we get that
|N^{\prime}|=|H_{1}^{\prime}||N_{2}^{\prime}|
where
H_{1}^{\prime}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n}
and
N_{2}^{\prime}\leq Q_{8}
. By
Lemma

7.8
, we have that
|H_{1}^{\prime}|=2^{a}3^{b}
where
a\geq 3b
. This implies that
|N_{2}^{\prime}|=1
and it follows that
N^{\prime}\unlhd\widetilde{T}^{n}
. It follows that

Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{r}=f(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}))\cong(\widetilde{T}^{n}/N^{\prime})\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}).

However, this implies that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{r}
has a normal subgroup of order
2^{3(n-t)}3^{n-t}
induced by
\widetilde{T}^{n}/N^{\prime}
. This contradicts
Lemma

7.10
.
∎

7.4.
Proof of
Theorem

7.1

Let
H=\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
and
G\in\MNEC_{B}(H)
. By Proposition
6.1
, there exists
Q\in\BPG
, a finite group
A
and representatives
f_{1}\in\widehat{\Quot}(H,A)
,
f_{2}\in\widehat{\Quot}(Q,A)
such that
G\cong H\times_{f_{1},f_{2}}Q
.
If
A=1
, then
G\cong H\times Q
. If
Q\cong\widetilde{T}
or
\widetilde{I}
, then we obtain
\widetilde{T}^{n+1}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
and
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m+1}
respectively.
If
Q\neq\widetilde{T},\widetilde{I}
, then
Q\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}
(by
Fig.

2
) and so there is a proper quotient
G\twoheadrightarrow H\times C_{2}
. Since
H\times C_{2}\twoheadrightarrow H
is non-Eichler, this implies that
G
is not minimal which is a contradiction.
If
A\cong Q
, then
G\cong H
which is again a contradiction.
If
A\neq 1,Q
, then
A\in\Quot(H)\cap\BPG^{*}\subseteq\{C_{3},A_{4},A_{5}\}
by
Proposition

7.5
. We will now consider each case in turn.

If
A=C_{3}
, then
Q=\widetilde{T}
(by
Fig.

2
). We have
\widehat{\Quot}(H,C_{3})=\{(f_{k},0)\}_{k=1}^{n}
(by
Proposition

7.5
) and
\widehat{\Quot}(\widetilde{T},C_{3})=\{f\}
.
This implies that

G\cong H\times_{(f_{k},0),f}\widetilde{T}\cong\widetilde{T}^{n-k}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(\widetilde{T}^{k}\times_{f_{k},f}\widetilde{T}).

Projection gives a split surjection
\widetilde{T}^{k}\times_{f_{k},f}\widetilde{T}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}^{k}
with kernel isomorphic to
\ker(\widetilde{T}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3})\cong Q_{8}
.
The action of
\widetilde{T}^{k}
on
Q_{8}
coincides with the action
\varphi:\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow C_{3}\leq\Aut(Q_{8})
defined previously and so
\widetilde{T}^{k}\times_{f_{k},f}\widetilde{T}\cong Q_{8}\rtimes_{\varphi}\widetilde{T}^{k}
. Hence
G\cong\widetilde{T}^{n-k}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})
for some
1\leq k\leq n
.

If
A=A_{4}
, then
Q=\widetilde{T}
(by
Fig.

2
). Since
\ker(\widetilde{T}\twoheadrightarrow A_{4})\cong C_{2}
,
Lemma

6.2
(iv) implies that

1\to C_{2}\to G\to H\to 1.

Since
\Aut(C_{2})=1
, there is a unique map
\psi:H\to\Aut(C_{2})
. By
Corollary

7.3
and
Proposition

7.4
, we have

\mathcal{E}(H,C_{2})\cong H^{2}(H,\mathbb{F}_{2})=0
.
This implies that
\mathcal{E}(H,C_{2})
contains only the trivial extension, and so
G\cong H\times C_{2}
.
If
A=A_{5}
, then
Q=\widetilde{I}
(by
Fig.

2
). Since
\ker(\widetilde{I}\twoheadrightarrow A_{5})\cong C_{2}
, we have that
G\cong H\times C_{2}
by the same argument.

Conversely, all groups obtained are non-Eichler covers of
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
and it can be shown that each group has no quotient in
B
. No groups are pairwise isomorphic: they each have distinct orders except the groups
\widetilde{T}^{n-k}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})
for
k=1,\cdots,n
which are distinct by
Proposition

7.11
. No groups have others as proper quotients:
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times C_{2}
does not quotient onto any other groups since it has the smallest order, and no groups have quotient
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times C_{2}
since they do not have
C_{2}
quotients (by
Lemma

7.6
). Similarly, by the order of the groups, none of the groups
\widetilde{T}^{n-k}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})
can have quotient
\widetilde{T}^{n+1}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
or
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m+1}
, and the converse follows from
Proposition

7.9
.

This completes the computation of
\MNEC_{B}(\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m})
.
It follows immediately that

\MNEC_{B}(\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m})=(\widetilde{\MNEC}_{B}(\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m}))^{\QF}=(\{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}\}\sqcup\{Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}:k\geq 1\})^{\QF}.

By
Proposition

7.11
, we have that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{r}
for all
r\neq k
, and
Lemma

7.6
implies that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
.
Hence
\MNEC_{B}(\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m})=\{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}\}\sqcup\{Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}:k\geq 1\}
.

8.
Final results

We will now discuss the various partial classification results which we obtain in light of the calculations done both via computer, which can be found in Appendix
A
, and for the infinite families which were dealt with in
Section

7
.

8.1.
Proof of
Theorem

E

We will now prove the following result from the introduction. We will make frequent use of the computer calculations which are presented in Appendix
A
.

Theorem
E
.

Let
G
be a finite group. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

G
has an Eichler quotient
H
of the form

C_{1},\,Q_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20},\,\widetilde{T},\,\widetilde{O},\,\widetilde{I}\quad\text{or}\quad\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\text{ for $n,m\geq 0$}

(ii)

G
has no quotient
H
of the form:

(a)

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6

(b)

Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
for
n\geq 1

(c)

Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
,
G_{(96,66)}
,
G_{(100,7)}
,
G_{(384,18129)}
,
G_{(1152,155476)}
.

Proof.

Let
S
denote the groups
H
listed in (i) above, i.e. the groups in
Eq.

1
.
The seven binary polyhedral groups listed, which we write as
\BPG
, are in
\MNEC(C_{1})
(by
Proposition

5.4
) and
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\in\MNEC^{n+m}(C_{1})
(by
Theorem

7.1
). In particular, we have
S\subseteq\mathscr{E}
.

To see that
S
is closed under quotients, suppose that there exists
G\in S
and
H\in\mathscr{E}\,\setminus\,S
with
G\twoheadrightarrow H
. The only reasonable option is
G=\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
for some
n,m\geq 0
.
Since
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptC_{2}
for
n,m\geq 0
(by
Proposition

7.5
), it follows that
H\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptC_{2}
and so
H\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486ptQ_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20},\,\widetilde{O}
. In particular,
H\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}
or
\widetilde{I}
. Let
a,b\geq 0
be such that
H\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}^{a}\times\widetilde{I}^{b}
and
a+b
is maximal. Then
H\not\in S
and
H\in\mathscr{E}
implies that
H\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}\in\MNEC(\widetilde{T}^{a}\times\widetilde{I}^{b})
. By assumption,
H\twoheadrightarrow\hskip-11.95013pt\parbox{2.84526pt}{/}\hskip 9.10486pt\widetilde{T}^{a+1}\times\widetilde{I}^{b}
,
\widetilde{T}^{a}\times\widetilde{I}^{b+1}
,
C_{2}
and so
H^{\prime}\cong\widetilde{T}^{a-k}\times\widetilde{I}^{b}\times(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k})
for some
1\leq k\leq a
by
Theorem

7.1
. This implies that
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
has a quotient
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
, which is a contradiction by
Proposition

7.9
.

By the Fundamental Lemma (
Lemma

1.5
), a finite group
G
has an Eichler quotient in
S
if and only if
G
has no quotient in
\MNEC(S)
.
Let
B=\{Q_{4n}:n\geq 6\}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
. If
G
has an Eichler quotient in
S
, then
G
has no quotient in
B
. It follows that a finite group
G
has an Eichler quotient in
S
if and only if
G
has no quotient in
B
or
\MNEC_{B}(S)
. It remains to determine

\MNEC_{B}(S)=\left\{(\MNEC_{B}(\BPG)\cup\MNEC_{B}(\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m}))\,\setminus\,(\BPG\cup\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m})\right\}^{\QF}

where
n,m\geq 0
and
(n,m)\neq(0,0)
, and equality follows from
Proposition

5.6
.

Firstly, we have
\MNEC_{B}(\BPG)=\MNEC_{B}(\G_{1})=\G_{2}
. By
Fig.

4
(or the tables in Appendix
A
), this consists of the groups listed in (ii) (c) as well as the groups
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{T}^{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times\widetilde{I}
and
\widetilde{I}^{2}
.

Secondly, by
Theorem

7.1
, we have

\MNEC_{B^{\prime}}(\{\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}\}_{n,m})=\{\,Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}:k\geq 1\,\}

where
B^{\prime}=B\cup\{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}\}\cup\{\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}\}
. In particular, we have

\MNEC_{B}(S)=\{(c)\cup\{\,Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}:k\geq 1\,\}\}^{\QF}

where (c) denote the sets of groups listed in (ii) (c). Firstly, note that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
has no quotient in (c) since
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
has no
C_{2}
quotient (by
Lemma

7.6
) and the groups in (c) all have
C_{2}
quotients (this follows from the BPG quotients arising in
Fig.

4
). The groups in (c) have no
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
quotient since
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}\in\G_{2}
(so they would have been filtered out otherwise). Since
|Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}|\geq 4608
for
k\geq 2
, they have no
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
quotients for any
k\geq 1
. Hence
\MNEC_{B}(S)
consists of the groups in (c) and the groups
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{k}
for
k\geq 1
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

E
.
∎

We can similarly deduce the following result from the introduction.

Theorem
1.8
.

Let
G
be a finite group such that
G\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

G
has an Eichler quotient
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}

(ii)

G
has no quotient of the form

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
,

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
,
(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T})\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}\times\widetilde{O}
or
\widetilde{T}^{2}\times C_{2}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

5.5
, it suffices to compute
\MNEC_{B}(H)
where
B=\{Q_{4n}:n\geq 6\}
. By
Fig.

4
, this consists of the groups
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
,
(Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T})\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}\times\widetilde{O}
and
\widetilde{T}^{2}\times C_{2}
.
∎

8.2.
Further partial classifications

We now consider two additional partial classifications.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
G
be a finite group with
|G|\leq 383
. Precisely one of the following holds.

(A)

G
has a quotient of the form

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
,

Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
or
G_{(100,7)}
(in which case
\mathbb{Z}G
does not have
\SFC
)

(B)

G\cong Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
or
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}

(C)

G
has an Eichler quotient
H
where
H
is one of the following groups:

(i)

C_{1},\,Q_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20},\,\widetilde{T},\,\widetilde{O},\,\widetilde{I}
(in which case
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
)

(ii)

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(96,66)}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the list of groups in
Eq.

1
as well as
\{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},G_{(96,66)},Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T},\widetilde{I}\times C_{2},\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}\}
, so that
S\subseteq\mathscr{E}
is closed under quotients. Let
B
be the quotient filter of the sets of groups listed in
Theorem

E
(ii) (a)-(c) as well as
\MNEC_{B}(H)
for each
H\in\{\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},G_{(96,66)},Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T},\widetilde{I}\times C_{2},\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}\}
where
B=\{Q_{4n}:n\geq 6\}
.
Define
\overline{S}=S\cap\{G:|G|\leq 383\}
and
\overline{B}=B\cap\{G:|G|\leq 383\}
.
If
G
is a finite group such that
|G|\leq 383
then, by the Fundamental Lemma (
Lemma

1.5
) and
Theorem

E
, either
G
has an Eichler quotient in
\overline{S}
or a quotient in
\overline{B}
(but not both).
Next note that
\overline{S}
consists of the groups in (B) and (C) (i)-(ii), and
\overline{B}
consists of the groups listed in (A). This requires that
\MNEC_{B}(G_{(96,66)})\cap\{G:|G|\leq 383\}=\emptyset
, which follows by a computer calculation.
Finally note that the groups in (B) have order
\geq 383/2
and so, if
G
has a quotient
H
in (B), then
G\cong H
.
∎

This reduces the complete classification of when PC holds for groups of order
\leq 383
to checking PC (resp. SFC) for
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
and
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
, and checking a weak version of PC lifting (resp. SFC lifting) for
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
and
G_{(96,66)}
.
By results of Bley-Hofmann-Johnston
[
BHJ24
]
(see
Theorem

1.6
), the groups
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}
and
\widetilde{T}\times Q_{12}
have SFC. By
Theorem

1.7
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
has SFC lifting.
By calculating group extensions, we can show that
G_{(192,183)}
is the unique group
G
with
|G|\leq 383
which has
G_{(96,66)}
as a proper Eichler quotient. By
Theorem

1.6
,
G_{(192,183)}
has SFC. Hence we obtain the following, which is one of the main results in
[
BHJ24
]
:

Corollary 8.2

(Bley-Hofmann-Johnston)
.

Let
G
be a finite group with
|G|\leq 383
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbb{Z}G
has stably free cancellation

(ii)

G
has no quotient of the form

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
,

Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
or
G_{(100,7)}
.

Remark 8.3
.

Note that we have given a proof that (ii) implies (i) using a slightly different approach to the one taken in
[
BHJ24
]
. Whilst they used the classification of groups of order
\leq 383
(using GAP’s Small Groups library
[
BEO02
,
GAP
]
), we proved the result as a consequence of
Theorem

E
.

We also have the following for
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
bounded. The proof is similar to that of
Theorem

8.1
except that we use the computations of
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
given in Appendix
A
. We also use that
Q_{8}\times C_{2}
and
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
are the only groups in
Theorem

E
(ii) (c) with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
.

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
. Precisely one of the following holds.

(A)

G
has a quotient of the form
Q_{8}\times C_{2}
or
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
(in which case
\mathbb{Z}G
does not have
\PC
)

(B)

G
has an Eichler quotient
H
where
H
is one of the following groups:

(i)

C_{1},\,Q_{8},\,Q_{12},\,Q_{16},\,Q_{20},\,\widetilde{T},\,\widetilde{O},\,\widetilde{I}
(in which case
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
)

(ii)

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{T}^{2}
.

This reduces the complete classification of when PC holds for such groups to checking PC for one group
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
(which has SFC by
Theorem

1.6
) and checking PC lifting for at most two groups
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
and
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
.
If it was known that
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}
had SFC lifting then, similarly to
Corollary

8.2
, we would have that a finite group
G
with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
has SFC if and only if
G
has no quotient of the form
Q_{8}\times C_{2}
or
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
.

In fact, since
m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)=2
for
H=Q_{8}\times C_{2},\,Q_{12}\times C_{2},\,\widetilde{T}\times C_{2},\,Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T},\,\widetilde{T}^{2}
, we obtain:

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
G
be a finite group with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 1
. Then
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
.

This generalises
[
Nic21a
, Theorem C]
which established the analogous result for
\SFC
.

8.3.
Proof of
Theorem

B

We will now prove the following result from the introduction.

Theorem
B
.

Let
G
be a finite group such that
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbb{Z}G
has projective cancellation

(ii)

\mathbb{Z}G
has stably free cancellation

(iii)

G
has no quotients of the form
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6
even,
Q_{4n}\times C_{2}
for
n=2,4
or
n\geq 3
odd,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
or
G_{(64,14)}

(iv)

G
has an Eichler quotient of the form
C_{2}^{2}
,
Q_{8}
or
Q_{16}
.

For a class of groups
A
, define
\mathscr{E}(A)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\MNEC^{n}(A)
which we will view as a directed graph with edge set
\{(G,H):G\in\MNEC(H)\}
where
(G,H)
is an edge from
G
to
H
.
We refer to the groups in
\mathscr{E}(A)
as
Eichler simple groups over
A
.

Similarly to Appendix
A
, we start by making a number of definitions. Let
\G_{0}(C_{2}^{2})=\{C_{2}^{2}\}
and
\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})=\MNEC(\G_{0}(C_{2}^{2}))
. If
B=\{Q_{4n}:\text{ $n\geq 6$ }\}
, then our GAP program can compute
\{G\in\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2}):\text{ $G$ has no quotient in $B$ }\}
. Note that
Q_{4n}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}
for all
n\geq 6
, but
Q_{4n}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}
if and only if
n
is even. If
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2},Q_{4n}
for
n
odd, then
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}\times_{C_{2}}Q_{4n}\cong Q_{4n}\times C_{2}
since we can choose generators for
C_{2}^{2}
such that the quotient map
C_{2}^{2}\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}
is projection. Combining this with the computations gives
\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
, which can be found in
Fig.

3
. (Note that technically the groups in
Q_{4n}
and
Q_{4n}\times C_{2}
for
n\geq 6
are not all in
\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
, but
(\,\cdot\,)^{\QF}
of this class.)

Let
B_{1}(C_{2}^{2})=\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})\,\setminus\,\{Q_{8},Q_{16}\}
. By
Fig.

4
(or Appendix
A
), these are the groups in
\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
which fail PC (and which fail SFC). Let
\G_{2}(C_{2}^{3})=\MNEC_{B_{1}(C_{2}^{2})}(\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2}))
. Again by
Fig.

4
, we have that
\G_{2}(C_{2}^{2})
consists of the four groups
Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{16}\times C_{2}
,
G_{(64,14)}
listed in
Fig.

3
.
All four groups fail PC (and SFC) by
Fig.

4
.

C_{2}^{2}

\quad Q_{8}\quad

\quad Q_{16}\quad

\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}

\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}

\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}^{2}

Q_{4n}
,
\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize$n\geq 6$}\\
\text{\normalsize even}\end{subarray}

Q_{4n}\times C_{2}
,
\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize$n\geq 3$}\\
\text{\normalsize odd}\end{subarray}

Q_{8}\times C_{2}

\,G_{(32,14)}\,

Q_{16}\times C_{2}

\,G_{(64,14)}\,

\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})

\G_{2}(C_{2}^{2})

Figure 3.
Graphical structure of
\G_{0}(C_{2}^{2})
,
\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
and
\G_{2}(C_{2}^{2})

Proof.

First note that (iv)
\Rightarrow
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) where the first arrow follows from
Theorem

D
and the third follows from
Fig.

4
. It therefore remains to prove that (iii) implies (iv). We will prove the contrapositive.
The Fundamental Lemma (
Lemma

1.5
) as stated only applies to
\mathscr{E}
and not
\mathscr{E}(C_{2}^{2})
, but we will use the same idea that went into its proof.

Let
G
be a finite group such that
G\twoheadrightarrow C_{2}^{2}
but which is not
H
-Eichler for
H=C_{2}^{2}
,
Q_{8}
or
Q_{16}
, i.e. (iv) is not satisfied.
Suppose for contradiction that (iii) is satisfied, i.e.
G
has no quotient which is one of the groups listed in (iii).
Since
G
is not
C_{2}^{2}
-Eichler,
Proposition

5.5
implies that
G\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime}\in\MNEC(C_{2}^{2})=\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
. By
Fig.

3
, all the groups in
\G_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
are listed in (iii) except
Q_{8}
,
Q_{16}
, and so
H^{\prime}=Q_{8}
,
Q_{16}
. By assumption,
G
is not
H^{\prime}
-Eichler and so
Proposition

5.5
implies that
G\twoheadrightarrow H^{\prime\prime}\in\MNEC(H^{\prime})
. Since
G
has no quotient in
B_{1}(C_{2}^{2})
, we have that
H^{\prime\prime}\in\MNEC_{B_{1}(C_{2}^{2})}(H^{\prime})
and so
H^{\prime\prime}
must have a quotient in
\MNEC_{B_{1}(C_{2}^{2})}(\{Q_{8},Q_{16}\})=\G_{2}(C_{2}^{2})
. By
Fig.

3
, all these groups are listed in (iii), which is a contradiction. Hence (iii) is not satisfied.
∎

8.4.
Groups with periodic cohomology

Recall that, if
P
is a (finitely generated) projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module, we say that
[P]\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
has
cancellation
if
P\oplus\mathbb{Z}G\cong P^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}G
implies
P\cong P^{\prime}
for all projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules
P^{\prime}
.
Let
D(\mathbb{Z}G)\leq\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
denote the kernel group.

We say that a finite group
G
has
periodic cohomology
if, for some
k\geq 1
, its Tate cohomology groups satisfy
\hat{H}^{i}(G;\mathbb{Z})=\hat{H}^{i+k}(G;\mathbb{Z})
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. We will now prove the following.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
G
be a finite group with periodic cohomology. Then
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\pc
if and only if
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
. Furthermore, if
P
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module, then:

(i)

If
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
, then
[P]
has cancellation

(ii)

If
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=3
, then:

(a)

If the Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G
is cyclic, then
[P]
has non-cancellation

(b)

If
[P]\in D(\mathbb{Z}G)
, then
[P]
has non-cancellation

(iii)

If
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\geq 4
, then
[P]
has non-cancellation.

Recall from
Section

5.1
that
\mathcal{B}(G)
is the set of equivalence classes, denote by
\sim
, of quotients
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
where
H
is a binary polyhedral group.
If
G
has periodic cohomology and
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(G)
, then
[
Nic20
, Corollary 1.4]
implies that
f_{1}\simeq f_{2}
if and only if
\IM(f_{1})\cong\IM(f_{2})
.
This shows that there is a bijection
\mathcal{B}(G)\cong\{H\text{ a binary polyhedral group}:G\twoheadrightarrow H\}
.
Given this, we will often write
H\in\mathcal{B}(G)
when there exists
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
with
f\in\mathcal{B}(G)
.
In order to determine
\mathcal{B}(G)
, it suffices to determine the set of maximal binary polyhedral quotients
\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)
which are the subset containing those
f\in\mathcal{B}(G)
such that
f
does not factor through any other
g\in\mathcal{B}(G)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove (i)-(iii) since this implies that
\mathbb{Z}G
has
\PC
if and only if
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
.
We begin by showing the result for
G
a binary polyhedral group (BPG). The BPGs with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
are
Q_{8}
,
Q_{12}
,
Q_{16}
,
Q_{20}
,
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{O}
,
\widetilde{I}
which all have
\PC
. Recall that
m_{\mathbb{H}}(Q_{4n})=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
.
The BPGs with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=3
are
Q_{24}
and
Q_{28}
.
For
Q_{24}
, the Sylow
2
-subgroup is
Q_{8}
(which is non-cyclic). The fact that
[P]
has non-cancellation whenever
[P]\in D(\mathbb{Z}Q_{24})
follows from close inspection of
[
Swa83
, Theorem III]
.
For
Q_{28}
, the Sylow
2
-subgroup is
C_{4}
and
\mathbb{Z}Q_{28}
has non-cancellation in every class by
[
Swa83
, Theorem I]
.
The BPGs with
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\geq 4
are
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 7
. Again by
[
Swa83
, Theorem I]
,
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
has non-cancellation in every class for
n\geq 7
.

We will now prove the general case by verifying it in three separate cases according to
\#\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)
.
First, if
\#\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)=0
, then
Proposition

5.2
implies that
G
is Eichler and so has
\PC
.

Next consider the case
\#\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)=1
. Let
f:G\twoheadrightarrow H
denote this quotient, with
H
a BPG. By
Proposition

5.2
, we have
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)
. If
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\leq 2
, then
m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)\leq 2
and so
H
is of the form
Q_{8},Q_{12},Q_{16},Q_{20},\widetilde{T},\widetilde{O},\widetilde{I}
and
G
has
\PC
by
Theorem

D
. This proves (i) in this case.
Now assume
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\geq 3
and let
P
be a projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module. If
P^{\prime}=f_{\#}(P)
and
[P^{\prime}]
has non-cancellation, then
[P]
has non-cancellation by
Theorem

2.7
. Hence, to verify
Theorem

8.6
, it suffices to show that cancellation fails for
[P^{\prime}]
in the cases (ii) and (iii).
Suppose
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)=3
. If the Sylow
2
-subgroup
\Syl_{2}(G)
is cyclic, then
\Syl_{2}(H)
is a quotient of
\Syl_{2}(G)
and so is cyclic. Since the result holds for BPGs, this implies that
[P^{\prime}]
has non-cancellation. If
[P]\in D(\mathbb{Z}G)
then, since
f_{\#}
induces a map
f_{\#}:D(\mathbb{Z}G)\to D(\mathbb{Z}H)
, we have
P^{\prime}=f_{\#}(P)\in D(\mathbb{Z}H)
. Similarly, this implies that
[P^{\prime}]
has non-cancellation which completes the proof of (ii). If
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=m_{\mathbb{H}}(H)\geq 4
, then
[P^{\prime}]
has non-cancellation by the result for BPGs and this completes the proof of (iii) in this case.

Finally, we consider the case
\#\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)>1
.
By
[
Nic20
, Theorems 1.5 & 1.10]
, we know that
\#\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)=2
or
3
. It can be shown (see the proof of
[
Nic20
, Theorem 1.10]
) that
\mathcal{B}_{\text{max}}(G)=\{Q_{8m_{1}},\cdots,Q_{8m_{b}}\}
where the
m_{i}
are odd coprime and

m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=\begin{cases}(m_{1}+m_{2})-1,&\text{if $b=2$}\\
(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})-2,&\text{if $b=3$}\end{cases}

depending on the two cases that arise. We will assume that
m_{1}<\cdots<m_{b}
.
Note that
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)=3
only when
b=2
,
m_{1}=1
and
m_{2}=3
. In this case,
G
has a quotient
Q_{24}
and so
\Syl_{2}(G)
is non-cyclic. Furthermore, if
P
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}G
module with
[P]\in D(\mathbb{Z}G)
, then
[P]
has non-cancellation by
Theorem

2.7
since its image lies in
D(\mathbb{Z}Q_{24})
under the extension of scalars map. In all other cases, we have
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)\geq 5
and
G
must have a quotient
Q_{8m}
for some
m\geq 5
. By the BPG case, we have that
\mathbb{Z}Q_{8m}
has non-cancellation in every class. By
Theorem

2.7
again,
\mathbb{Z}G
has non-cancellation in every class. This completes the proof.
∎

In contrast to (ii), we have the following which follows from
[
Swa83
, Theorem III]
.

Proposition 8.7
.

The quaternion group
Q_{24}
of order
24
has
m_{\mathbb{H}}(Q_{24})=3
and a (non-cyclic) Sylow
2
-subgroup
Q_{8}
. Furthermore,
[\mathbb{Z}G]
has non-cancellation but there exists a projective
\mathbb{Z}Q_{24}
module
P
with
[P]\not\in D(\mathbb{Z}Q_{24})
for which
[P]
has cancellation.

A.
Computation of Eichler simple groups

Let
\mathscr{E}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\MNEC^{n}(\{C_{1}\})
and let
B\subseteq\mathscr{E}
denote the subset consisting of the groups:

(i)

Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 6

(ii)

Q_{8}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{12}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{16}\times C_{2}
,
Q_{20}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}
,
\widetilde{O}\times C_{2}
,
\widetilde{I}\times C_{2}^{2}

(iii)

G_{(32,14)}
,
G_{(36,7)}
,
G_{(64,14)}
,
G_{(100,7)}
.

These are the groups in
\mathscr{E}
which, by
[
Swa83
,
Che86
,
BHJ24
]
, are currently known to fail PC and have no proper quotients in
\mathscr{E}
which fail PC.
Let
B_{1}=\{\,Q_{4n}:n\geq 6\,\}
and, for each
n\geq 2
, let
B_{n}=B_{n-1}\cup(B\cap\MNEC^{n}(\{C_{1}\})
. For each
n\geq 1
, define
\G_{n}=\MNEC^{n}_{B_{n}}(\{C_{1}\})
and
\G=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\G_{n}
.
We have
B_{2}=B\,\setminus\,\{\,\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}^{2}\,\}
and
B_{n}=B
for all
n\geq 3
.
We compute
\G_{0}
,
\G_{1}
and
\G_{2}
completely as well as those groups
G\in\G_{3}
which have a quotient
H\in\G_{2}
currently known to have PC, i.e.
\widetilde{T}\times C_{2}
and
\widetilde{T}^{n}\times\widetilde{I}^{m}
for
n+m=2
. We present the list of groups in both a diagram and a table.

Diagram of groups

In
Fig.

4
, we sketch
\mathcal{G}=\G_{0}\sqcup\G_{1}\sqcup\G_{2}\sqcup\G_{3}^{\prime}
where
\G_{3}^{\prime}\subseteq\G_{3}
is the subset defined above. If
G,H\in\mathcal{G}
and
G\in\MNEC(H)
, we draw a line from
G
to
H
. Three of the lines are dashed to indicate that they pass between groups in

and

to
Q_{12}\in\G_{1}
.

A.2.
Table of groups

The column headings are as follows.

1.

MNEC ID.

We name each group by an MNEC ID
(n,m)
which refers to the
m
th group in
\G_{n}
. The corresponding group is denoted by
\G_{(n,m)}
.
The ordering is chosen so that
|\G_{(n,m)}|\leq|\G_{(n,m^{\prime})}|
for
m\leq m^{\prime}
, but otherwise follows no specific pattern. For each
n\leq 3
, the groups
\G_{(n,1)},\G_{(n,2)},\ldots
coincide with groups in the diagram listed from top to bottom.

2.

Group ID / Order.
Where possible, we will list the Small Group ID
(n,m)
which refers to the
m
th group of order
n
in GAP’s Small Groups library
[
BEO02
,
GAP
]
. The corresponding group is denoted by
G_{(n,m)}
. Otherwise, we just list the order of the group.

3.

Edges To.
For a group
G\in\G_{n}
, we list the MNEC IDs of all groups
H\in\G_{m}
for which
G\in\MNEC(H)
, i.e. they are connected by an edge in
\mathscr{E}
. Note that this determines all quotients between groups in
\mathcal{G}
since they correspond to directed paths in
\mathscr{E}
(see
Proposition

5.9
).

4.

m
H
(
\bm{G}
). We compute
m_{\mathbb{H}}(G)
using
Proposition

5.3
.

5.

Description.

For groups which are direct products of previously defined groups, we list the groups (see
Section

7
for the definition of
Q_{8}\rtimes\widetilde{T}^{n}
).
We give descriptions to the groups (32,14), (36,7), (64,14), (96,66), (100,7), (384, 18129) and (1152,155476).

6.

Cancellation.

We record whether
G
is known to have PC, known to fail SFC, or is known to have SFC but with PC currently unknown. If we have no information, we write a dash.
